The **Democratic Party** is a [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_liberalism_in_the_United_States) political party in the United States, sitting on the [center](39619035.md) to [center-left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-left_politics) of the [political spectrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_spectrum). Founded in 1828, it is the world's oldest active political party. Its main rival is the [Republican Party](32070.md), and since the 1850s, both have dominated [American politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_politics).

The Democratic Party initially supported [Jacksonian democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacksonian_democracy), [agrarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarianism), and [geographical expansionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manifest_destiny), while opposing [a national bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_War) and high [tariffs](55551.md). Democrats won six of the eight presidential elections from 1828 to 1856, losing twice to the [Whigs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whig_Party_\(United_States\)). In 1860, the party split into [Northern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Democrats) and [Southern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Democrats) factions over [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States). The party remained dominated by agrarian interests, contrasting with Republican support for the [big business](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_business) of the [Gilded Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilded_Age). A Democratic candidate won the presidency only twice[^cite_note-8] between 1860 and 1908, although Democratic candidates won the popular vote two more times in that period.[^cite_note-9] During the [Progressive Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_Era), some [factions of the party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Factions_in_the_Democratic_Party_\(United_States\)) supported [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism_in_the_United_States) reforms, with [Woodrow Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson) being elected president in 1912 and 1916.

In 1932, [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) was elected president after campaigning on a strong response to the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression). His [New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal) programs created [a broad Democratic coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal_coalition) that united white Southerners, Northern workers, [labor unions](2474406.md), [African Americans](2154.md), [Catholic](410868.md) and [Jewish communities](1131183.md), progressives, and [liberals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_liberalism_in_the_United_States). From the late 1930s, [a conservative minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_coalition) in the party's Southern wing joined with Republicans as a reaction to progressive domestic reforms.[^cite_note-Zeitz-2023-10] After the [civil rights movement](49001.md) and [Great Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Society) era of progressive legislation under [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson), who was often able to overcome the conservative coalition in the 1960s, many white Southerners [switched to the Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_strategy) as the Northeastern states became more reliably Democratic.[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-gallup2010-12] The party's labor union element has weakened since the 1970s amid [deindustrialization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deindustrialization), and during the 1980s it lost many white [working-class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_class) voters to the Republicans under [Ronald Reagan](25433.md). The election of [Bill Clinton](3356.md) in 1992 marked a shift for the party toward [centrism](39619035.md) and the [Third Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Way), shifting its economic stance toward [market-based policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberalism).[^cite_note-Hale-1995-13][^cite_note-Wills-1997-14][^cite_note-15] [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) oversaw passage of the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act) in 2010, which passed along party lines.[^cite_note-16][^cite_note-17]

On economic issues, the party favors [healthcare reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_reform_in_the_United_States), [paid sick leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paid_sick_leave), [paid family leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paid_family_leave) and [supporting unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unionization).[^cite_note-Miranda_Ollstein-2022-18][^cite_note-Goodnough-2019-19][^cite_note-20][^cite_note-jlevy-21][^cite_note-U.S._Department_of_State-22] On social issues, it advocates for [abortion rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_abortion-rights_movement),[^cite_note-Traister-2023-23] [gun control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_control_in_the_United_States),[^cite_note-24] [LGBTQ rights](3510770.md),[^cite_note-NPR-2012a-25] action on [climate change](5042951.md),[^cite_note-26] the [legalization of marijuana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legality_of_cannabis_by_U.S._jurisdiction), and a pathway to citizenship for [undocumented immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undocumented_immigrants_\(U.S.\)).[^cite_note-THE_2020_DEMOCRATIC_PLATFORM-27][^cite_note-The_American_Presidency_Project-2020-28] Since 2016, the party has seen a significant growth in support for [progressivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-29] In foreign policy, the party supports [liberal internationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_internationalism) and [aid to Ukraine](70196881.md), as well as tough stances against China and Russia.

## History

Main article: [History of the Democratic Party (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Democratic_Party_\(United_States\))

Democratic Party officials often trace its origins to the [Democratic-Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party), founded by [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson), [James Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Madison), and other influential opponents of the conservative [Federalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalist_Party) in 1792.[^cite_note-US_Congress-1991-30][^cite_note-31] That party died out before the modern Democratic Party was organized;[^cite_note-32] the Jeffersonian party also inspired the [Whigs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whig_Party_\(United_States\)) and modern Republicans.[^cite_note-33] Historians argue that the modern Democratic Party was first organized in the late 1820s with the election of war hero [Andrew Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Jackson)[^cite_note-Kazin-2022-34] of Tennessee, making it the world's oldest active political party.[^cite_note-Lucas-2014-35][^cite_note-Janda-2010-36][^cite_note-Kazin-2022-34] It was predominately built by [Martin Van Buren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Van_Buren) who assembled a wide cadre of politicians in every state behind Jackson.[^cite_note-Lucas-2014-35][^cite_note-Kazin-2022-34]

Since the nomination of [William Jennings Bryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Jennings_Bryan) in 1896, the party has generally positioned itself to the left of the [Republican Party](32070.md) on economic issues, though [Progressive Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_Republican) persisted until the 1930s. Democrats have been more liberal on civil rights since 1948, though conservative factions within the Democratic Party that opposed them persisted in the South until the 1960s. On foreign policy, both parties have changed positions several times.[^cite_note-37]

### Background



[Andrew Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Jackson) was the seventh president (1829–1837) and the first Democratic president.

The Democratic Party evolved from the [Democratic-Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party) organized by [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson) and [James Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Madison) in opposition to the [Federalist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalist_Party).[^cite_note-38] The Democratic-Republican Party favored [republicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism_in_the_United_States), a weak [federal government](195149.md), [states' rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States'_rights), agrarian interests (especially Southern planters), and strict adherence to the Constitution. The party opposed a national bank and [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain).[^cite_note-39] After the [War of 1812](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_1812), the Federalists virtually disappeared and the only national political party left was the Democratic-Republicans, which was prone to splinter along regional lines.[^cite_note-40] The era of one-party rule in the United States, known as the [Era of Good Feelings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Era_of_Good_Feelings), lasted from 1816 until 1828, when Andrew Jackson became president. Jackson and Martin Van Buren worked with allies in each state to form a new Democratic Party on a national basis. In the 1830s, the [Whig Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whig_Party_\(United_States\)) coalesced into the main rival to the Democrats.

When exactly the Democratic Party formed is still debated among historians, with many putting forth the 1828 date of the creation of a federal structure for the various Jacksonian movements as the foundation date, however, it could also be argued that the foundation of these Jacksonian groups could be the foundation date. In that case, the Democratic Party would be formed on December 23, 1823, when the Greensburg Committee read the *Greensburg Resolution* outside the [Westmoreland County Courthouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westmoreland_County_Courthouse) in [Greensburg, Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greensburg,_Pennsylvania). The committee consisted of five of Greensburg's most prominent political figures, the brothers [Jacob M. Wise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_M._Wise) (state senator), [John H. Wise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_H._Wise_\(Pennsylvania_politician\)) (state representative and brigadier general), and Frederick A. Wise (owner and editor of the *[Westmoreland Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westmoreland_Republican)*), alongside [David Marchand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Marchand) (state representative), and [James Clarke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Clarke_\(Pennsylvania_politician\)) (state representative). The *Greensburg Resolution* was the first published call for Jackson to run for president with the committee being the first overtly "Jacksonian" organization, dubbed the 'origin' of the Jackson movement that turned into the Democratic Party.[^cite_note-41]

The event that transformed the Jacksonians from just another faction of the Democratic-Republican party into a divergent political force would be the so-called "[corrupt bargain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corrupt_bargain)" of 1824, where, despite his winning the most popular and electoral votes, the House of Representatives did not confirm Jackson as the newly elected president, instead [Henry Clay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Clay), who was both a candidate and the speaker of the house, [whipped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whip_\(politics\)) his supporters in congress to vote for the runner-up, [John Quincy Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams), in exchange for Adams's naming Clay the Secretary of State. Jackson and his followers began to coalesce more seriously into a structured party for the next election in 1828, and the party's birth is most often marked as [Jackson's campaign launch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Jackson_1828_presidential_campaign) on [January 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Eighth_\(United_States\)) of that year,[^cite_note-42][^cite_note-43][^cite_note-44] or sometimes as four years later at the inaugural [1832 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1832_Democratic_National_Convention).[^cite_note-45]

Before 1860, the Democratic Party supported [expansive presidential power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_executive_theory),[^cite_note-Holt-1992-46] [the interests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_Power) of [slave states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_states_and_free_states),[^cite_note-Bates-2015-47] [agrarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarianism),[^cite_note-EB-2023-48] and [expansionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expansionism),[^cite_note-EB-2023-48] while opposing [a national bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_War) and high [tariffs](55551.md).[^cite_note-EB-2023-48]

### 19th century

#### Jacksonian Era

Further information: [Second Party System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Party_System) and [Third Party System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Party_System)



[Martin Van Buren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Van_Buren) was the eighth president (1837–1841).

&#x20;The [Democratic-Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party) split over the choice of a successor to President [James Monroe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Monroe).[^cite_note-49] The faction that supported many of the old [Jeffersonian principles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffersonian_democracy), led by Andrew Jackson and Martin Van Buren, became the modern Democratic Party.[^cite_note-50] Historian [Mary Beth Norton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Beth_Norton) explains the transformation in 1828:&#x20;

> Jacksonians believed the people's will had finally prevailed. Through a lavishly financed coalition of state parties, political leaders, and newspaper editors, a popular movement had elected the president. The Democrats became the nation's first well-organized national party ... and tight party organization became the hallmark of nineteenth-century American politics.[^cite_note-51]



[James K. Polk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_K._Polk) was the 11th president (1845–1849). He significantly extended the territory of the United States.

&#x20;Behind the platforms issued by state and national parties stood a widely shared political outlook that characterized the Democrats:[^cite_note-Zeitz-2023-10]

> The Democrats represented a wide range of views but shared a fundamental commitment to the Jeffersonian concept of an agrarian society. They viewed the central government as the enemy of individual liberty. The [1824 "corrupt bargain"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corrupt_bargain#Election_of_1824) had strengthened their suspicion of Washington politics. ... Jacksonians feared the concentration of economic and political power. They believed that government intervention in the economy benefited special-interest groups and created corporate monopolies that favored the rich. They sought to restore the independence of the individual—the artisan and the ordinary farmer—by ending federal support of banks and corporations and restricting the use of paper currency, which they distrusted. Their definition of the proper role of government tended to be negative, and Jackson's political power was largely expressed in negative acts. He exercised the veto more than all previous presidents combined. ... Nor did Jackson share reformers' humanitarian concerns. He had no sympathy for American Indians, initiating the removal of the Cherokees along the [Trail of Tears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trail_of_Tears).[^cite_note-52]

Opposing factions led by [Henry Clay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Clay) helped form the Whig Party. The Democratic Party had a small yet decisive advantage over the Whigs until the 1850s when the Whigs fell apart over the issue of slavery. In 1854, angry with the [Kansas–Nebraska Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas%E2%80%93Nebraska_Act), anti-slavery Democrats left the party and joined Northern Whigs to form the [Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_United_States_Republican_Party).[^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54] Martin van Buren also helped found the [Free Soil Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Soil_Party) to oppose the spread of slavery, running as its candidate in the [1848 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1848_United_States_presidential_election), before returning to the Democratic Party and staying loyal to the Union.[^cite_note-55]

#### American Civil War



[Stephen A. Douglas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_A._Douglas) was a United States senator for Illinois.

The Democrats split over slavery, with Northern and Southern tickets in the [election of 1860](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1860_United_States_presidential_election), in which the Republican Party gained ascendancy.[^cite_note-56] The radical pro-slavery [Fire-Eaters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire-Eaters) led walkouts at the two conventions when the delegates would not adopt a resolution supporting the extension of slavery into territories even if the voters of those territories did not want it. These [Southern Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1860_United_States_presidential_election#Constitutional_\(Southern\)_Democratic) nominated the pro-slavery incumbent vice president, [John C. Breckinridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_C._Breckinridge) of Kentucky, for president and General [Joseph Lane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Lane), of Oregon, for vice president. The [Northern Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1860_United_States_presidential_election#National_\(Northern\)_Democratic) nominated Senator [Stephen A. Douglas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_A._Douglas) of Illinois for president and former Georgia Governor [Herschel V. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herschel_Vespasian_Johnson) for vice president. This fracturing of the Democrats led to a Republican victory and [Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln) was elected the 16th president of the United States.[^cite_note-57]

As the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) broke out, Northern Democrats were divided into [War Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_Democrats) and [Peace Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copperheads_\(politics\)). The [Confederate States of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America) deliberately avoided organized political parties. Most War Democrats rallied to Republican president Abraham Lincoln and the Republicans' [National Union Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Union_Party_\(United_States\)) in the [election of 1864](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1864_United_States_presidential_election), which featured [Andrew Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Johnson) on the Union ticket to attract fellow Democrats. Johnson replaced Lincoln in 1865, but he stayed independent of both parties.[^cite_note-58]

#### Reconstruction and Redemption

The Democrats benefited from white Southerners' resentment of [Reconstruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_era_of_the_United_States) after the war and consequent hostility to the Republican Party. After [Redeemers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redeemers) ended Reconstruction in the 1870s, and following the often extremely violent [disenfranchisement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disfranchisement_after_the_Reconstruction_era) of African Americans led by such [white supremacist](33178.md#Academic_use_of_the_term) Democratic politicians as [Benjamin Tillman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Tillman) of [South Carolina](27956.md) in the 1880s and 1890s, the South, voting Democratic, became known as the "[Solid South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solid_South)". Although Republicans won all but two presidential elections, the Democrats remained competitive. The party was dominated by pro-business [Bourbon Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourbon_Democrat) led by [Samuel J. Tilden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_J._Tilden) and [Grover Cleveland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grover_Cleveland), who represented mercantile, banking, and railroad interests; opposed [imperialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialism) and overseas expansion; fought for the [gold standard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_standard); opposed [bimetallism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bimetallism); and crusaded against corruption, high taxes and tariffs. Cleveland was elected to non-consecutive presidential terms in [1884](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1884_United_States_presidential_election) and [1892](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1892_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-59]

### 20th century

Further information: [Fourth Party System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Party_System), [Fifth Party System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Party_System), and [Sixth Party System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_Party_System)

#### Progressive era



Leaders of the Democratic Party during the first half of the 20th century in 1913: William Jennings Bryan, [Josephus Daniels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephus_Daniels), [Woodrow Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson), [Breckinridge Long](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breckinridge_Long), [William Phillips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Phillips_\(diplomat\)), and Franklin D. Roosevelt.

Agrarian Democrats demanding [free silver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_silver), drawing on Populist ideas, overthrew the Bourbon Democrats in 1896 and nominated [William Jennings Bryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Jennings_Bryan) for the presidency (a nomination repeated by Democrats in 1900 and 1908). Bryan waged a vigorous campaign attacking Eastern moneyed interests, but he lost to Republican [William McKinley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_McKinley).[^cite_note-60]

The Democrats took control of the House in 1910, and [Woodrow Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson) won election as president in 1912 (when the Republicans split) and 1916. Wilson effectively led Congress to put to rest the issues of tariffs, money, and antitrust, which had dominated politics for 40 years, with new progressive laws. He failed to secure Senate passage of the [Versailles Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Versailles_Treaty) (ending the war with Germany and joining the League of Nations).[^cite_note-61] The weakened party was deeply divided by issues such as the KKK and prohibition in the 1920s. However, it did organize new ethnic voters in Northern cities.[^cite_note-62]

After [World War I](4764461.md) ended and continuing through the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression), the Democratic and Republican Parties both largely believed in [American exceptionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_exceptionalism) over European monarchies and [state socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_socialism) that existed elsewhere in the world.[^cite_note-63]

#### 1930s–1960s and the rise of the New Deal coalition



Franklin D. Roosevelt and Harry S. Truman, the 32nd and 33rd presidents (1933–1945; 1945–1953), featured on a campaign poster for the [1944 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1944_United_States_presidential_election); note the rooster logo of the Democratic Party (see [Names and Symbols](#Name_and_symbols) below).

The [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) in 1929 that began under Republican president [Herbert Hoover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Hoover) and the Republican Congress set the stage for a more liberal government as the Democrats controlled the House of Representatives nearly uninterrupted from 1930 until 1994, the Senate for 44 of 48 years from 1930, and won most presidential elections until 1968. [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt), elected to the presidency in 1932, came forth with federal government programs called the [New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal). New Deal liberalism meant the regulation of business (especially finance and banking) and the promotion of labor unions as well as federal spending to aid the unemployed, help distressed farmers, and undertake large-scale public works projects. It marked the start of the American welfare state.[^cite_note-64] The opponents, who stressed opposition to unions, support for business and low taxes, started calling themselves "conservatives".[^cite_note-65]

Until the 1980s, the Democratic Party was a coalition of two parties divided by the Mason–Dixon line: liberal Democrats in the North and culturally conservative voters in the South, who, though benefiting from many of the New Deal public-works projects, opposed increasing [civil rights](37071.md) initiatives advocated by Northeastern liberals. The polarization grew stronger after Roosevelt died. Southern Democrats formed a key part of the bipartisan [conservative coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_coalition) in an alliance with most of the Midwestern Republicans. The economically activist philosophy of Franklin D. Roosevelt, which has strongly influenced [American liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism_in_the_United_States), shaped much of the party's economic agenda after 1932.[^cite_note-66] From the 1930s to the mid-1960s, the liberal [New Deal coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal_coalition) usually controlled the presidency while the conservative coalition usually controlled Congress.[^cite_note-67]

#### 1960s–1980s and the collapse of the New Deal coalition

See also: [Civil rights movement](49001.md)

Issues facing parties and the United States after World War II included the [Cold War](325329.md) and the [civil rights movement](49001.md). Republicans attracted conservatives and, after the 1960s, white Southerners from the Democratic coalition with their use of the [Southern strategy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_strategy) and resistance to New Deal and [Great Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Society) liberalism. Until the 1950s, African Americans had traditionally supported the Republican Party because of its anti-slavery civil rights policies. Following the passage of the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964) and [Voting Rights Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act_of_1965), the Southern states became more reliably Republican in presidential politics, while Northeastern states became more reliably Democratic.[^cite_note-Regional_Variations_in_the_Realignment_of_American_Politics,_1944–2004-68][^cite_note-69][^cite_note-Black-2003-70][^cite_note-Activists_and_Partisan_Realignment-71] Studies show that Southern whites, who were a core constituency in the Democratic Party, shifted to the Republican Party due to [racial backlash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_backlash) and [social conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism).[^cite_note-Issue_Evolution-72][^cite_note-Valentino-2005-73][^cite_note-74]



[John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy), the 35th president (1961–1963)



[Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson), the 36th president (1963–1969)

The election of President [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy) from Massachusetts in 1960 partially reflected this shift. In the campaign, Kennedy attracted a new generation of younger voters. In his agenda dubbed the [New Frontier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Frontier), Kennedy introduced a host of social programs and public works projects, along with enhanced support of the [space program](18426568.md), proposing a crewed spacecraft [trip to the moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_11) by the end of the decade. He pushed for civil rights initiatives and proposed the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964), but with his [assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_John_F._Kennedy) in November 1963, he was not able to see its passage.[^cite_note-75]

Kennedy's successor [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) was able to persuade the largely conservative Congress to pass the Civil Rights Act of 1964, and with a more progressive Congress in 1965 passed much of the [Great Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Society), including [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(United_States\)) and [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid), which consisted of an array of social programs designed to help the poor, sick, and elderly. Kennedy and Johnson's advocacy of civil rights further solidified black support for the Democrats but had the effect of alienating Southern whites who would eventually gravitate toward the Republican Party, particularly after the election of [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) to the presidency in 1980. Many conservative [Southern Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Democrats) defected to the [Republican Party](32070.md), beginning with the passage of the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964) and the general leftward shift of the party.[^cite_note-76][^cite_note-Black-2003-70][^cite_note-Activists_and_Partisan_Realignment-71][^cite_note-Valentino-2005-73]

The United States' involvement in the [Vietnam War](32611.md) in the 1960s was another divisive issue that further fractured the fault lines of the Democrats' coalition. After the [Gulf of Tonkin Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Tonkin_Resolution) in 1964, President Johnson committed a large contingency of combat troops to Vietnam, but the escalation failed to drive the [Viet Cong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viet_Cong) from South Vietnam, resulting in an increasing [quagmire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quagmire_theory), which by 1968 had become the subject of widespread anti-war protests in the United States and elsewhere. With increasing casualties and nightly news reports bringing home troubling images from Vietnam, the costly military engagement became increasingly unpopular, alienating many of the kinds of young voters that the Democrats had attracted in the early 1960s. The protests that year along with assassinations of [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md) and Democratic presidential candidate Senator [Robert F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Kennedy) (younger brother of John F. Kennedy) climaxed in turbulence at the hotly-contested [Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Democratic_National_Convention) that summer in Chicago (which among the ensuing turmoil inside and outside of the convention hall nominated Vice President [Hubert Humphrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubert_Humphrey)) in a series of events that proved to mark a significant turning point in the decline of the Democratic Party's broad coalition.[^cite_note-77]



[Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter), the 39th president (1977–1981)

Republican presidential nominee [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) was able to capitalize on the confusion of the Democrats that year and won the 1968 election to become the 37th president. He won re-election in a [landslide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_United_States_presidential_election) in 1972 against Democratic nominee [George McGovern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_McGovern), who like Robert F. Kennedy, reached out to the younger anti-war and counterculture voters, but unlike Kennedy, was not able to appeal to the party's more traditional white working-class constituencies.[^cite_note-78] During Nixon's second term, his presidency was rocked by the [Watergate scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watergate_scandal), which forced him to resign in 1974. He was succeeded by vice president [Gerald Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Ford), who served a brief tenure.

Watergate offered the Democrats an opportunity to recoup, and their nominee [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) won the 1976 presidential election. With the initial support of [evangelical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical) Christian voters in the South, Carter was temporarily able to reunite the disparate factions within the party, but inflation and the [Iran Hostage Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_Hostage_Crisis) of 1979–1980 took their toll, resulting in a [landslide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_United_States_presidential_election) victory for Republican presidential nominee [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) in 1980, which shifted the political landscape in favor of the Republicans for years to come. The influx of conservative Democrats into the Republican Party is often cited as a reason for the Republican Party's shift further to the right during the late 20th century as well as the shift of its base from the Northeast and Midwest to the South.[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80]

#### 1990s and Third Way centrism



[Bill Clinton](3356.md), the 42nd president (1993–2001)

With the ascendancy of the Republicans under Ronald Reagan, the Democrats searched for ways to respond yet were unable to succeed by running traditional candidates, such as former vice president and Democratic presidential nominee [Walter Mondale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Mondale) and Massachusetts governor [Michael Dukakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Dukakis), who lost to Reagan and [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) in the [1984](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_United_States_presidential_election) and [1988 presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_United_States_presidential_election), respectively. Many Democrats attached their hopes to the future star of [Gary Hart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Hart), who had challenged Mondale in the [1984 primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries) running on a theme of "New Ideas"; and in the subsequent [1988 primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries) became the de facto front-runner and virtual "shoo-in" for the Democratic presidential nomination before a sex scandal ended his campaign. The party nevertheless began to seek out a younger generation of leaders, who like Hart had been inspired by the pragmatic idealism of John F. Kennedy.[^cite_note-81]

Arkansas governor [Bill Clinton](3356.md) was one such figure, who was [elected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_United_States_presidential_election) president in 1992 as the Democratic nominee. The [Democratic Leadership Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Leadership_Council) was a campaign organization connected to Clinton that advocated a [realignment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_realignment) and [triangulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triangulation_\(politics\)) under the re-branded "[New Democrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Democrats_\(United_States\))" label.[^cite_note-Geismer-2019-82][^cite_note-Hale-1995-13][^cite_note-Wills-1997-14] The party adopted a synthesis of [neoliberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberalism) [economic policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_policies) with [cultural liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_liberalism), with the voter base after Reagan having shifted considerably to the [right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_\(politics\)).[^cite_note-Geismer-2019-82] In an effort to appeal both to liberals and to fiscal conservatives, Democrats began to advocate for a [balanced budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balanced_budget) and [market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy) tempered by [government intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_interventionism) ([mixed economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy)), along with a continued emphasis on [social justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_justice) and [affirmative action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative_action). The economic policy adopted by the Democratic Party, including the former [Clinton administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Bill_Clinton), has been referred to as "[Third Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Way)".

The Democrats lost control of Congress in the [1994 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Revolution) to the Republicans. However, in [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_United_States_Presidential_Election), Clinton was re-elected; he was the first Democratic president since Franklin D. Roosevelt to win a second full term. In December 1998, Republicans in the House of Representatives [impeached Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_of_Bill_Clinton) for his role in the [Clinton–Lewinsky scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton%E2%80%93Lewinsky_scandal), but was [acquitted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_trial_of_Bill_Clinton) by the Senate in February 1999.[^cite_note-83] Clinton's vice president [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore) ran to succeed him as president, and won the popular vote, but after a controversial election dispute over a Florida recount settled by the [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md) (which [ruled 5–4 in favor of Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bush_v._Gore)), he lost the [2000 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_Presidential_Election) to Republican opponent [George W. Bush](3414021.md) in the [Electoral College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College).[^cite_note-Cornell-BushvGore-84]

### 21st century



[Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama), the 44th president (2009–2017)

In the wake of the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md) and the growing concern over [global warming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_warming), some of the party's key issues in the early 21st century have included combating [terrorism](30636.md) while preserving human rights, expanding access to health care, labor rights, and environmental protection.

In the [2006 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_general_elections,_2006), Democrats regained majority control of both the House and the Senate, and in 2008, [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) won the Democratic Party's nomination and was elected as the first [African-American president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_heritage_of_presidents_of_the_United_States#Barack_Obama). Under the Obama presidency, the party moved forward with reforms including an [economic stimulus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Recovery_and_Reinvestment_Act_of_2009) package, the [Dodd–Frank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dodd-Frank_Act) financial reform act, and, in its biggest impact, [reshaped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_reform_in_the_United_States) U.S. [healthcare](27553159.md) with the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act).[^cite_note-85][^cite_note-Dayen_2024-86]

#### 2000s–2010s and the Obama era

In the [2010 midterm elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_elections), the Democratic Party lost control of the House as well as its majorities in several state legislatures and governorships. The 2010 elections also marked the end of the Democratic Party's electoral dominance in the [Southern United States](179553.md).[^cite_note-The_long_goodbye-87]

In the [2012 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_United_States_presidential_election), President Obama was re-elected, but the party remained in the minority in the House of Representatives and lost control of the Senate in the [2014 midterm elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_United_States_elections). After the [2016 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election) of [Donald Trump](4848272.md), who lost the popular vote to Democratic nominee [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton), the Democratic Party transitioned into the role of an opposition party and held neither the presidency nor Congress for two years.[^cite_note-Revolt_of_the_Rust_Belt-88] However, the party won back the House in the [2018 midterm elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections) under the leadership of [Nancy Pelosi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Pelosi).

Democrats were extremely critical of President Trump, particularly his policies on immigration, healthcare, as well as his response to the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md).[^cite_note-89][^cite_note-90] In December 2019, Democrats in the House of Representatives [impeached Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_impeachment_of_Donald_Trump), although he was acquitted in the Republican-controlled Senate.[^cite_note-91]

#### 2020s and opposition to Trumpism



[Joe Biden](145422.md), the 46th president (2021–2025)

In November 2020, Democratic candidate [Joe Biden](145422.md) defeated Trump to win the [2020 presidential election](48410011.md).[^cite_note-92] Biden's running mate, [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris), was the [first female](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_female_United_States_presidential_and_vice_presidential_candidates) and [person of African and South Asian descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African-American_United_States_presidential_and_vice_presidential_candidates) to become vice president in history. He began his term with extremely narrow Democratic majorities in the U.S. House and Senate.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94] During the Biden presidency, the party was characterized as adopting an increasingly [progressive economic agenda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_progressivism).[^cite_note-Bridging_the_Blue_Divide-95] In 2022, Biden appointed [Ketanji Brown Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketanji_Brown_Jackson), the [first black woman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_American_federal_judges#United_States_Supreme_Court) on the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States). However, she was replacing liberal justice [Stephen Breyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Breyer), thus she did not alter the court's 6–3 split between conservatives (the majority) and liberals.[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97][^cite_note-98][^cite_note-99] After *[Dobbs v. Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization)* (decided June 24, 2022), which led to [abortion bans in much of the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_law_in_the_United_States_by_state), the Democratic Party rallied behind [abortion rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion-rights_movements).[^cite_note-Traister-2023-23]

In the [2022 midterm elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_States_elections), Democrats dramatically outperformed historical trends and a widely anticipated [red wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wave_elections_in_the_United_States) did not materialize.[^cite_note-Tumulty_2022-100][^cite_note-Blake_20222-101] The party only narrowly lost its majority in the U.S. House and expanded its majority in the U.S. Senate,[^cite_note-Kinery_2022-102][^cite_note-Enter_2022-103][^cite_note-Crampton_2022-104] along with several gains at the state level.[^cite_note-composition_2023_05_23_ncsl_org-105][^cite_note-statehouse_2023_01_18_nytimes-106][^cite_note-trifectas_2022_11_11_ap_foxnews-107][^cite_note-states_2023_07_01_gazette-108]

In July 2024, after [a series of age and health concerns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_and_health_concerns_about_Joe_Biden), Biden [withdrew from the presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_Joe_Biden_from_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election), becoming the first incumbent president since [Lyndon B. Johnson in 1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_Lyndon_B._Johnson_from_the_1968_United_States_presidential_election) to withdraw from running for reelection, the first since the 19th century to withdraw after serving only one term,[^cite_note-111] and the only one to ever withdraw after already winning [the primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries).[^cite_note-Klassen-2024-109][^cite_note-112]

Vice President Kamala Harris—who became Biden's replacement on the ballot after his withdrawal from the race—became the [first black woman to be nominated by a major party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African-American_United_States_presidential_and_vice_presidential_candidates#U.S._presidential_candidates:_party_nominees), but she was defeated in the [2024 election](59568546.md) by Donald Trump. Harris lost the [electoral college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_electoral_college) 312–226 (including all seven of the anticipated [swing states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swing_state)) as well as the popular vote, becoming the first Democratic candidate to do so since [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry) in 2004, amid what was a global anti-incumbent backlash.[^cite_note-graveyard-113][^cite_note-114][^cite_note-Global_Politics-115][^cite_note-116]

#### Current status

As of 2026, Democrats hold 24 [state governorships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_governors), 17 [state legislatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_state_legislatures), 16 state government [trifectas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_trifecta), and the mayorships in the majority of the country's major cities.[^cite_note-117] Three of the nine current [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md) justices were appointed by Democratic presidents. By registered members, the Democratic Party is the largest party in the U.S. and the [third largest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_political_parties). All totaled, 16 Democrats have served as president of the United States.[^cite_note-sarnold-118]

## Name and symbols

"Democratic donkey" redirects here. For the statue, see [*Democratic Donkey* (statue)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Donkey_\(statue\)).

|  | It has been suggested that mascots be [**split**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Splitting) out into another article. ([**Discuss**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Political_parties_in_the_United_States#Article_on_party_mascots)) *(August 2024)* |
| --- | --- |



"A Live Jackass Kicking a Dead Lion" by Thomas Nast, *[Harper's Weekly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harper's_Magazine)*, January 19, 1870.



The donkey party logo remains a well-known symbol for the Democratic Party despite not being the official logo of the party.



In use since 2010, the letter "D" enclosed in a circle has been one of the Democratic Party's main symbols.

The [Democratic-Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party) splintered in 1824 into the short-lived [National Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Republican_Party) and the Jacksonian movement which in 1828 became the Democratic Party. During the Jacksonian era, the term "The Democracy" was in use by the party, but the name "Democratic Party" was eventually settled upon[^cite_note-119] and became the official name in 1844.[^cite_note-120] Members of the party are called "Democrats" or "Dems".

The most common mascot symbol for the party has been the donkey, or jackass.[^cite_note-121] [Andrew Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Jackson)'s enemies twisted his name to "jackass" as a term of ridicule regarding a stupid and stubborn animal. However, the Democrats liked the common-man implications and picked it up too, therefore the image persisted and evolved.[^cite_note-122] Its most lasting impression came from the cartoons of [Thomas Nast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Nast) from 1870 in *[Harper's Weekly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harper's_Magazine)*. Cartoonists followed Nast and used the donkey to represent the Democrats and the elephant to represent the Republicans.



In many states, the logo of the Democratic Party was a rooster, for instance, in Alabama: Logo of the [Alabama Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Democratic_Party), 1904–1966 (left) and 1966–1996 (right).

[^cite_note-Ingram-123]

[^cite_note-Bad_symbol_removed-124]

In the early 20th century, the traditional symbol of the Democratic Party in Indiana, Kentucky, Oklahoma, and Ohio was the rooster, as opposed to the Republican eagle.[^cite_note-Barbour_County-2008-125] The rooster was also adopted as an official symbol of the national Democratic Party.[^cite_note-126] In 1904, the Alabama Democratic Party chose, as the logo to put on its ballots, a rooster with the motto "White supremacy – For the right."[^cite_note-127] The words "White supremacy" were replaced with "Democrats" in 1966.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-Ingram-123] In 1996, the Alabama Democratic Party dropped the rooster, citing racist and white supremacist connotations linked with the symbol.[^cite_note-Bad_symbol_removed-124] The rooster symbol still appears on Oklahoma, Kentucky, Indiana, and West Virginia [ballots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballot).[^cite_note-Barbour_County-2008-125] In New York, the Democratic ballot symbol is a five-pointed star.[^cite_note-129]

Although both major political parties (and many minor ones) use the traditional American colors of red, white, and blue in their marketing and representations, since the [election night 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_presidential_election) blue has become the identifying color for the Democratic Party while red has become the identifying color for the Republican Party. That night, for the first time all major broadcast television networks used the same color scheme for the electoral map: [blue states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_states_and_blue_states) for [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore) (Democratic nominee) and red states for [George W. Bush](3414021.md) (Republican nominee). Since then, the color blue has been widely used by the media to represent the party. This is contrary to common practice outside of the United States where blue is the traditional color of the right and red the color of the left.[^cite_note-WP_Nov_2004-130]

In 2010, the party introduced a new logo, with a blue capital "D" enclosed in a blue circle.[^cite_note-131] It remained the party's primary brand mark, with minor changes to the shade of blue, for 15 years. In 2025, a new logo was introduced, which incorporates a white donkey facing to the right instead of the left, with three blue stars in the center instead of four, on a blue background.[^cite_note-132]

[Jefferson-Jackson Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson-Jackson_Day) is the annual fundraising event (dinner) held by Democratic Party organizations across the United States.[^cite_note-133] It is named after presidents [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson) and [Andrew Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Jackson), whom the party regards as its distinguished early leaders.

The song "[Happy Days Are Here Again](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Happy_Days_Are_Here_Again)" is the unofficial song of the Democratic Party. It was used prominently when [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) was nominated for president at the [1932 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1932_Democratic_National_Convention) and remains a sentimental favorite for Democrats. For example, [Paul Shaffer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Shaffer) played the theme on the *[Late Show with David Letterman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Show_with_David_Letterman)* after the Democrats won Congress in 2006. "[Don't Stop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_Stop_\(Fleetwood_Mac_song\))" by [Fleetwood Mac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fleetwood_Mac) was adopted by [Bill Clinton](3356.md)'s presidential campaign in 1992 and has endured as a popular Democratic song. The emotionally similar song "[Beautiful Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beautiful_Day)" by the band [U2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U2) has also become a favorite theme song for Democratic candidates. [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry) used the song during his 2004 presidential campaign and several Democratic congressional candidates used it as a celebratory tune in 2006.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135]

As a traditional anthem for its presidential nominating convention, [Aaron Copland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaron_Copland)'s "[Fanfare for the Common Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fanfare_for_the_Common_Man)" was performed at the beginning of the [1976 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_Democratic_National_Convention).[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137]

## Structure



Democratic National Headquarters (2024)

### National committee

Main article: [Democratic National Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_National_Committee)

Further information: [List of Democratic National Conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Democratic_National_Conventions) and [List of chairs of the Democratic National Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_chairs_of_the_Democratic_National_Committee)

The [Democratic National Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_National_Committee) (DNC) is responsible for promoting Democratic campaign activities. While the DNC is responsible for overseeing the process of writing the Democratic Platform, the DNC is more focused on campaign and organizational strategy than [public policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_policy). In presidential elections, it supervises the [Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_National_Convention). The national convention is subject to the charter of the party and the ultimate authority within the Democratic Party when it is in session, with the DNC running the party's organization at other times. Since February 1, 2025, the DNC has been chaired by [Ken Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Martin).[^cite_note-138]

### State parties

Main article: [List of state parties of the Democratic Party (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_state_parties_of_the_Democratic_Party_\(United_States\))

Each state also has a state committee, made up of elected committee members as well as ex officio committee members (usually elected officials and representatives of major constituencies), which in turn elects a chair. County, town, city, and ward committees generally are composed of individuals elected at the local level. State and local committees often coordinate campaign activities within their jurisdiction, oversee local conventions, and in some cases primaries or caucuses, and may have a role in nominating candidates for elected office under state law. Rarely do they have much direct funding, but in 2005 DNC Chairman Dean began a program (called the "50 State Strategy") of using DNC national funds to assist all state parties and pay for full-time professional staffers.[^cite_note-139]

In addition, state-level party committees operate in the territories of [American Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Samoa_Democratic_Party), [Guam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_Guam), and [Virgin Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_the_Virgin_Islands), the commonwealths of [Northern Mariana Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_\(Northern_Mariana_Islands\)) and [Puerto Rico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_\(Puerto_Rico\)), and the [District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Democratic_State_Committee), with all but Puerto Rico being active in nominating candidates for both presidential and territorial contests, while Puerto Rico's Democratic Party is organized only to nominate presidential candidates. The [Democrats Abroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democrats_Abroad) committee is organized by American voters who reside outside of U.S. territory to nominate presidential candidates. All such party committees are accorded recognition as state parties and are allowed to elect both members to the National Committee as well as delegates to the National Convention.

### Major party committees and groups

Further information: [Democratic Party (United States) organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_\(United_States\)_organizations)



Then-Senator Obama speaking to College Democrats of America in 2007.

The [Democratic Congressional Campaign Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Congressional_Campaign_Committee) (DCCC) assists party candidates in House races and is chaired by Representative [Suzan DelBene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzan_DelBene) of [Washington](13015878.md). Similarly, the [Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Senatorial_Campaign_Committee) (DSCC), chaired by Senator [Gary Peters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Peters) of Michigan, raises funds for Senate races. The [Democratic Legislative Campaign Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Legislative_Campaign_Committee) (DLCC), chaired by [Majority Leader of the New York State Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majority_Leader_of_the_New_York_State_Senate) [Andrea Stewart-Cousins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrea_Stewart-Cousins), is a smaller organization that focuses on state legislative races. The [Democratic Governors Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Governors_Association) (DGA) is an organization supporting the candidacies of Democratic gubernatorial nominees and incumbents. Likewise, the mayors of the largest cities and urban centers convene as the [National Conference of Democratic Mayors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Conference_of_Democratic_Mayors).[^cite_note-140]

The DNC sponsors the [College Democrats of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Democrats_of_America) (CDA), a student-outreach organization with the goal of training and engaging a new generation of Democratic activists. [Democrats Abroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democrats_Abroad) is the organization for Americans living outside the United States. They work to advance the party's goals and encourage Americans living abroad to support the Democrats. The [Young Democrats of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Democrats_of_America) (YDA) and the [High School Democrats of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_School_Democrats_of_America) (HSDA) are young adult and youth-led organizations respectively that attempt to draw in and mobilize young people for Democratic candidates but operate outside of the DNC.

## Political positions

Main article: [Political positions of the Democratic Party (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_positions_of_the_Democratic_Party_\(United_States\))



This graph shows the real median [U.S. household income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Household_income_in_the_United_States) by race: 1967 to 2011, in 2011 dollars.

[^cite_note-Income_by_Race-141]

The Democratic Party is widely described in [American sources](5392786.md) as either a [centrist](39619035.md)[^cite_note-142] or a [center-left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-left_politics) political party.[^cite_note-Brookings2019-143] In international comparisons, some have observed that the party's platform is most similar to center-left parties such as the [Social Democratic Party of Germany](60585.md), the [Ensemble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ensemble_\(political_coalition\)) in France, or the [Liberal Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_\(UK\)) and the [Labour Party](19279158.md) in the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-nyt2019-144][^cite_note-Prospect2024-145] The party also contains distinct [left-wing](18499.md) subgroups (such as the "[Squad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squad_\(U.S._Congress\))") alongside more moderate coalitions within its broad electoral coalition.[^cite_note-Prospect2024-145][^cite_note-Brookings2021-146][^cite_note-FiveThirtyEight2019-147] Political scientists Robert C. Sinclair and R. Jeffrey Melton described the Democratic Party as "slightly to the right of the largest Canadian party, the center-left [Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Liberal_Party)".[^cite_note-148]

The 21st-century Democratic Party is unique and differs from other parties of similar profile in its ideological orientation, in part due to its [heterogenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homogeneity_and_heterogeneity) demographic composition. In particular, the Democratic Party's ideology derives from being supported by both racial minorities, particularly [African Americans](2154.md), as well as white voters with high [educational attainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Educational_attainment_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-Harry_Enten-149][^cite_note-Nate_Silver-150] Ideologically, the Democratic Party is more diverse than the [Republican Party](32070.md), according to data collected by [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup,_Inc.).[^cite_note-151]

Its voting demographics are heavily educationally and racially-polarized, but not income polarized.[^cite_note-culture_trumps_economic_class-152] The Democratic Party is weakest among white voters without college degrees and [white evangelicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicalism_in_the_United_States) in the 21st century.[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155] Higher educational attainment is strongly correlated with higher income and wealth, and also strongly correlated with increased ideological support for the Democratic Party's positions among white voters.[^cite_note-Abramowitz_2021-156]

This derives in part from unique regional characteristics of the United States, particularly the [Southern United States](179553.md). Racial polarization is extremely high in the Southern United States, with [black Southerners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Southerners) almost entirely voting for the Democratic Party, and [white Southerners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Southerners) almost entirely voting for the Republican Party.[^cite_note-Cohn-2014-157][^cite_note-Dogs-158] Also, white Southerners with college degrees are strongly Republican, unlike in most of the rest of the country.[^cite_note-White_Vote_and_Educational_Polarization-159] [African Americans](2154.md) continue to have the lowest incomes of any racial group in the United States.[^cite_note-Income_by_Race-141]

The Democratic Party's contemporary liberalism has its origins in the [Puritans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puritans) of [New England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England), with their emphasis on education and science dating back to the colonial era and the [Scientific Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_Revolution). This liberalism is older than the [classical liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_liberalism) or [social democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_democracy) of the 19th century.[^cite_note-160]

The Democratic Party's social positions derive from those of the [New Left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Left), that is [cultural liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_liberalism). These include [feminism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feminism), [LGBTQ rights](3510770.md), [drug policy reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drug_liberalization), and [environmentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmentalism).[^cite_note-Issues-161][^cite_note-Carmines_and_Layman-162][^cite_note-Kaufman-163][^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165] The party's platform favors a generous [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state) and a greater measure of social and economic equality.[^cite_note-Larry_E._Sullivan_2009_p_291-166] On social issues, it advocates for [the continued legality of abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion-rights_movements),[^cite_note-Traister-2023-23] the [legalization of marijuana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legality_of_cannabis_by_U.S._jurisdiction),[^cite_note-Gurley-2020-167] a pathway to citizenship for [undocumented immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undocumented_immigrants_\(U.S.\)),[^cite_note-THE_2020_DEMOCRATIC_PLATFORM-27][^cite_note-The_American_Presidency_Project-2020-28] and LGBTQ rights.[^cite_note-NPR-2012a-25]

### Economic issues

The [social safety net](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_safety_net) and strong [labor unions](2474406.md) have been at the heart of Democratic economic policy since the [New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal) in the 1930s.[^cite_note-Larry_E._Sullivan_2009_p_291-166] The Democratic Party's economic policy positions, as measured by votes in Congress, tend to align with those of the middle class.[^cite_note-Grossmann-2021-168][^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172] Democrats support a [progressive tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_tax) system, higher [minimum wages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage_in_the_United_States), [equal opportunity employment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_opportunity_employment), [Social Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Security_\(United_States\)), [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care), [public education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_the_United_States), and [subsidized housing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsidized_housing_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-Larry_E._Sullivan_2009_p_291-166] They also support [infrastructure development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrastructure_Investment_and_Jobs_Act) and clean energy investments to achieve economic development and job creation.[^cite_note-173]

Throughout the 1970s and 1990s, the party has at times supported "[Third Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Way)" [centrist](39619035.md) economic reforms that cut the size of government and [reduced market regulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deregulation), as seen through the policies of presidents [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) and [Bill Clinton](3356.md).[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175] The party has generally rejected both [*laissez-faire* economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laissez-faire) and [market socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_socialism), instead favoring [Keynesian economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keynesian_economics) within a [regulated capitalist market-based system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy).[^cite_note-176] It broadly opposes [right-to-work legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-to-work_legislation)[^cite_note-177] and supports pro-union laws such as the [Protecting the Right to Organize Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protecting_the_Right_to_Organize_Act),[^cite_note-178] and favors increasing the [minimum wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage_in_the_United_States) to at least \$15 per hour.[^cite_note-179] However, the party is not [social democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_democratic) and does not base its policies on organized labor.[^cite_note-180] However, since the 2020s, it has also been distancing itself from predistributive economic policies such as job guarantees, minimum wage increases, and pro-union legislation.[^cite_note-181]

Historically, the Democratic Party opposed [tariffs](55551.md) and favored [free trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade).[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183] However, since the 2020s, the party has moved in favor of [protectionistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionism) policies.[^cite_note-184]

#### Fiscal policy

Democrats support a more [progressive tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_tax) structure to provide more services and reduce [economic inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality) by making sure that the wealthiest Americans pay more in taxes.[^cite_note-how-high-185] Democrats and Republicans traditionally take differing stances on eradicating poverty. Brady said "Our poverty level is the direct consequence of our weak social policies, which are a direct consequence of weak political actors".[^cite_note-186] They oppose the cutting of social services, such as [Social Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Security_\(United_States\)), [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(United_States\)), and [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid),[^cite_note-187] believing it to be harmful to efficiency and [social justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_justice). Democrats believe the benefits of social services in monetary and non-monetary terms are a more [productive labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Productive_and_unproductive_labour) force and cultured population and believe that the benefits of this are greater than any benefits that could be derived from lower taxes, especially on top earners, or cuts to social services. Furthermore, Democrats see social services as essential toward providing [positive freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Positive_liberty), freedom derived from economic opportunity. The Democratic-led House of Representatives reinstated the [PAYGO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PAYGO) (pay-as-you-go) budget rule at the start of the [110th Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/110th_United_States_Congress).[^cite_note-188]

#### Minimum wage

See also: [Minimum wage in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage_in_the_United_States)

The Democratic Party favors raising the [minimum wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage). The [Fair Minimum Wage Act of 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_Minimum_Wage_Act_of_2007) was an early component of the Democrats' agenda during the [110th Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/110th_United_States_Congress). In 2006, the Democrats supported six state-ballot initiatives to increase the minimum wage and all six initiatives passed.[^cite_note-democrats.org-189]

In 2017, Senate Democrats introduced the Raise the Wage Act which would raise the minimum wage to \$15 an hour by 2024.[^cite_note-190] In 2021, Democratic president [Joe Biden](145422.md) proposed increasing the minimum wage to \$15 by 2025.[^cite_note-191] In many states with Democratic legislative and gubernatorial control, the state minimum wage has been increased to a rate above the federal minimum wage, usually to \$15 per hour or higher.[^cite_note-192]

#### Health care



President Obama signing the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act) into law in 2010.

Democrats call for "affordable and quality health care" and favor moving toward [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care) in a variety of forms to address rising healthcare costs. Progressive Democratic politicians favor a [single-payer program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-payer_health_care) or [Medicare for All](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_for_All), while liberals prefer creating a [public health insurance option](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_health_insurance_option).[^cite_note-Goodnough-2019-19]

The [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act), signed into law by President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) on March 23, 2010, has been one of the most significant pushes for universal health care. As of December 2019, more than 20 million Americans have gained health insurance under the Affordable Care Act.[^cite_note-193]

#### Education

Democrats favor improving [public education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_education) by raising school standards and reforming the [Head Start program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_Start_\(program\)). They also support [universal preschool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_preschool), expanding access to primary education, including through [charter schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_schools), and are generally opposed to [school voucher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_voucher) programs. They call for addressing [student loan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_loan) debt and reforms to reduce college tuition.[^cite_note-194] Other proposals have included tuition-free public universities and reform of [standardized testing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standardized_testing). Democrats have the long-term aim of having publicly funded college education with low tuition fees (like in much of Europe and Canada), which would be available to every eligible American student. Alternatively, they encourage expanding access to post-secondary education by increasing state funding for student financial aid such as [Pell Grants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pell_Grants) and [college tuition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_tuition) [tax deductions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_deductions).[^cite_note-195]

#### Environment

Main article: [Environmental policy of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_policy_of_the_United_States)



Democrats and Republicans have diverged on the seriousness of the threat posed by climate change, with Democrats' assessment rising significantly in the mid-2010s.

[^cite_note-PewClimateChange_20230418-196]



The sharp divide over the existence of and responsibility for global warming and climate change falls largely along political lines. Overall, 60% of those surveyed said oil and gas companies were "completely or mostly responsible" for climate change.

[^cite_note-Guardian_20211026-197]



Opinion about human causation of climate change increased substantially with education among Democrats, but not among Republicans.

[^cite_note-Pew_20220301-198]

&#x20;Conversely, opinions favoring becoming carbon neutral declined substantially with age among Republicans, but not among Democrats.

[^cite_note-Pew_20220301-198]



A broad range of policies to reduce greenhouse gas emissions has been proposed. Democrats' support for such policies consistently exceeds that of Republicans.

[^cite_note-Gallup_20220411-199]



Acceptance of wind and solar facilities in one's community is stronger among Democrats (blue), while acceptance of nuclear power plants is stronger among Republicans (red).

[^cite_note-WashPost_20231003-200]

Democrats believe that the government should protect the environment and have a history of environmentalism. In more recent years, this stance has emphasized [renewable energy](25784.md) generation as the basis for an improved economy, greater [national security](240468.md), and general environmental benefits.[^cite_note-201] The Democratic Party is substantially more likely than the Republican Party to support environmental regulation and policies that are supportive of renewable energy.[^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203]

The Democratic Party also favors expansion of conservation lands and encourages open space and rail travel to relieve highway and airport congestion and improve air quality and the economy as it "believe\[s] that communities, environmental interests, and the government should work together to protect resources while ensuring the vitality of local economies. Once Americans were led to believe they had to make a choice between the economy and the environment. They now know this is a false choice".[^cite_note-204]

The foremost environmental concern of the Democratic Party is [climate change](5042951.md). Democrats, most notably former vice president [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore), have pressed for stern regulation of [greenhouse gases](21350772.md). On [October 15, 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Nobel_Peace_Prize), Gore won the [Nobel Peace Prize](26230922.md) for his efforts to build greater knowledge about man-made climate change and lay the foundations for the measures needed to counteract it.[^cite_note-205]

#### Renewable energy and fossil fuels

Democrats have supported increased domestic [renewable energy](25784.md) development, including wind and solar power farms, in an effort to reduce carbon pollution. The party's platform calls for an "all of the above" energy policy including clean energy, natural gas, and domestic oil, with the desire of becoming energy independent.[^cite_note-democrats.org-189] The party has supported higher taxes on [oil companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_companies) and increased regulations on [coal power plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal-fired_power_station), favoring a policy of reducing long-term reliance on [fossil fuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuels).[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207] Additionally, the party supports stricter [fuel emissions standards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuel_emissions_standard) to prevent air pollution.

During his presidency, Joe Biden enacted the [Inflation Reduction Act of 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_Reduction_Act_of_2022), which is the largest allocation of funds for [addressing climate change](2119179.md) in the history of the United States.[^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209][^cite_note-210]

#### Trade

Like the Republican Party, the Democratic Party has taken widely varying views on [international trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_trade) throughout its history. The Democratic Party has usually been more supportive of [free trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade) than the Republican Party.[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-Stokes-2016-212][^cite_note-CEPR-2018-213][^cite_note-214]

The Democrats dominated the [Second Party System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Party_System) and set low tariffs designed to pay for the government but not protect industry. Their opponents, the Whigs, wanted high protective tariffs but usually were outvoted in Congress. Tariffs soon became a major political issue as the [Whigs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whig_Party_\(United_States\)) (1832–1852) and (after 1854) the Republicans wanted to protect their mostly Northern industries and constituents by voting for higher tariffs and the [Southern Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Democrats), which had very little industry but imported many goods voted for lower tariffs. After the Second Party System ended in 1854, the Democrats lost control and the new Republican Party had its opportunity to raise rates.[^cite_note-215]

During the [Third Party System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Party_System), Democratic president [Grover Cleveland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grover_Cleveland) made low tariffs the centerpiece of Democratic Party policies, arguing that high tariffs were an unnecessary and unfair tax on consumers.
