The South and [West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_United_States) generally supported low tariffs, while the industrial [North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_United_States) supported high tariffs.[^cite_note-216] During the [Fourth Party System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Party_System), Democratic president [Woodrow Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson) made a drastic lowering of tariff rates a major priority for his presidency. The 1913 [Underwood Tariff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Underwood_Tariff) cut rates, and the new revenues generated by the [federal income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_income_tax) made tariffs much less important in terms of economic impact and political rhetoric.[^cite_note-217]

During the [Fifth Party System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Party_System), the [Reciprocal Tariff Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reciprocal_Tariff_Act) of 1934 was enacted during [FDR's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) administration, marking a sharp departure from the era of [protectionism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionism_in_the_United_States). American duties on foreign products declined from an average of 46% in 1934 to 12% by 1962.[^cite_note-Bailey-218] After World War II, the U.S. promoted the [General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Agreement_on_Tariffs_and_Trade) (GATT) established in 1947 during the [Truman administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Harry_S._Truman), to minimize tariffs liberalize trade among all capitalist countries.[^cite_note-barton-219][^cite_note-220]

In the 1990s, the Clinton administration and several prominent Democrats pushed through several agreements such as the [North American Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement) (NAFTA).[^cite_note-221] [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) signed several free trade agreements during his presidency while [Joe Biden](145422.md) did not sign any free trade agreements during his presidency and increased some tariffs on China.[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223]

During Republican Donald Trump's two terms as president, the Democratic Party has been more in favor of free trade than the Republican Party.[^cite_note-Stokes-2016-212][^cite_note-CEPR-2018-213] The Democratic Party remains supportive of the [USMCA](58620732.md) free trade agreement with Mexico and Canada.[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225]

### Social issues



[Shirley Chisholm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirley_Chisholm) was the [first black candidate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_candidates_for_President_of_the_United_States#Shirley_Chisholm) for a major-party nomination for President of the United States.

The modern Democratic Party emphasizes [social equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_equality) and [equal opportunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_opportunity). Democrats support [voting rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_rights_in_the_United_States) and [minority rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_rights), including [LGBT rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-Issues-161][^cite_note-226][^cite_note-227][^cite_note-228] Democratic president [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) signed the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964), which outlawed racial segregation. Carmines and Stimson wrote "the Democratic Party appropriated racial liberalism and assumed federal responsibility for ending racial discrimination."[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231]

Ideological social elements in the party include [cultural liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_liberalism), [civil libertarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_libertarianism), and [feminism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feminism).[^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234] Some Democratic social policies are immigration reform, [electoral reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_reform), and women's [reproductive rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproductive_rights).[^cite_note-235][^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237]

#### Equal opportunity

The Democratic Party is a staunch supporter of [equal opportunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_opportunity) for all Americans regardless of sex, age, race, ethnicity, [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation), [gender identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_identity), religion, creed, or national origin. The Democratic Party has broad appeal across most socioeconomic and ethnic demographics, as seen in recent exit polls.[^cite_note-238] Democrats also strongly support the [Americans with Disabilities Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americans_with_Disabilities_Act_of_1990) to prohibit discrimination against people based on physical or mental disability. As such, the Democrats pushed as well the [ADA Amendments Act of 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ADA_Amendments_Act_of_2008), a disability rights expansion that became law.[^cite_note-239]

Most Democrats support [affirmative action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative_action) to further equal opportunity. However, in 2020 [57% voters in California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_California_Proposition_16) voted to keep their state constitution's ban on affirmative action, despite Biden [winning 63% of the vote in California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_California) in the same election.[^cite_note-240]

#### Voting rights

The party is very supportive of improving "voting rights" as well as election accuracy and accessibility.[^cite_note-241] They support extensions of voting time, including making election day a holiday. They support reforming the electoral system to eliminate [gerrymandering](12987.md), abolishing the [electoral college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College), as well as passing comprehensive [campaign finance reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campaign_finance_reform_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-ontheissues.org-242]

#### Abortion and reproductive rights

See also: [Abortion in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_the_United_States)

The Democratic position on abortion has changed significantly over time.[^cite_note-Williams-2015-243][^cite_note-Williams-2022b-244] During the late 1960s and early 1970s, Republicans generally favored legalized abortion more than Democrats,[^cite_note-Halpern-2018-245] although significant heterogeneity could be found within both parties.[^cite_note-Taylor-2018-246] During this time, opposition to abortion tended to be concentrated within the political left in the United States. Liberal Protestants and Catholics (many of whom were Democratic voters) opposed abortion, while most conservative Protestants supported legal access to abortion services.[^cite_note-Williams-2015-243][^cite_note-247]

In its national platforms from 1992 to 2004, the Democratic Party has called for abortion to be "safe, legal and rare"—namely, keeping it legal by rejecting laws that allow governmental interference in abortion decisions and reducing the number of abortions by promoting both knowledge of reproduction and contraception and incentives for adoption. When Congress voted on the [Partial-Birth Abortion Ban Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partial-Birth_Abortion_Ban_Act) in 2003, congressional Democrats were split, with a minority (including former [Senate majority leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_leaders_of_the_United_States_Senate) [Harry Reid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Reid)) supporting the ban and the majority of Democrats opposing the legislation.[^cite_note-248]

According to the 2020 Democratic Party platform, "Democrats believe every woman should be able to access high-quality reproductive health care services, including safe and legal abortion."[^cite_note-249]

After *[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)* (1973) was overturned in *[Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization)* (2022), Democratic-controlled states and ballot initiatives were able to ensure access to abortion. The number of abortions in the United States increased after *Dobbs*, due to the [right to travel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_movement_under_United_States_law) between states.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251]

#### Immigration

See also: [Immigration to the United States](15051.md) and [Illegal immigration to the United States](5044573.md)

Further information: [History of immigration to the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_immigration_to_the_United_States)



Apprehensions at the southwest border since 2001.

[^cite_note-IllegalCrossings_2001--252]

Like the Republican Party, the Democratic Party has taken widely varying views on immigration throughout its history. Since the 1990s, the Democratic Party has been more supportive overall of immigration than the Republican Party.[^cite_note-253] Many Democratic politicians have called for systematic reform of the immigration system such that residents that have [come into the United States illegally](5044573.md) have a pathway to legal citizenship. President Obama remarked in November 2013 that he felt it was "long past time to fix our broken immigration system," particularly to allow "incredibly bright young people" who came over as students to become full citizens.[^cite_note-long-past-254] In 2013, Democrats in the Senate passed [S. 744](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Security,_Economic_Opportunity,_and_Immigration_Modernization_Act_of_2013), which would reform immigration policy to allow citizenship for illegal immigrants in the United States. The law failed to pass in the House and was never re-introduced after the [113th Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/113th_Congress).[^cite_note-255]

Opposition to immigration has increased in the 2020s, with a majority of Democrats supporting increasing border security.[^cite_note-256][^cite_note-257]

#### LGBTQ rights

See also: [LGBTQ rights in the United States](3510770.md)

The Democratic position on [LGBTQ rights](3510770.md) has changed significantly over time.[^cite_note-Igielnik-2022-258][^cite_note-Lindberg-2022-259] Before the 2000s, like the Republicans, the Democratic Party often took positions hostile to LGBTQ rights. As of the 2020s, both voters and elected representatives within the Democratic Party are overwhelmingly supportive of [LGBT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT) rights.[^cite_note-Igielnik-2022-258]

Support for same-sex marriage has steadily increased among the general public, including voters in both major parties, since the start of the 21st century. An April 2009 ABC News/*Washington Post* public opinion poll put support among Democrats at 62%.[^cite_note-260] A 2006 [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) poll of Democrats found that 55% supported gays adopting children with 40% opposed while 70% support [gays in the military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation_and_military_service), with only 23% opposed.[^cite_note-261] Gallup polling from May 2009 stated that 82% of Democrats support open enlistment.[^cite_note-262] A 2023 Gallup public opinion poll found 84% of Democrats support same-sex marriage, compared to 71% support by the general public and 49% support by Republicans.[^cite_note-263]

The 2004 Democratic National Platform stated that marriage should be defined at the state level and it repudiated the [Federal Marriage Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Marriage_Amendment).[^cite_note-platform-264] [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry), the Democratic presidential nominee in 2004, did not support same-sex marriage in [his campaign.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry_2004_presidential_campaign) While not stating support of same-sex marriage, the 2008 platform called for repeal of the [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act), which banned federal recognition of same-sex marriage and removed the need for interstate recognition, supported antidiscrimination laws and the extension of hate crime laws to LGBTQ people and opposed "don't ask, don't tell".[^cite_note-UCLA_press-265][^cite_note-266] The 2012 platform included support for same-sex marriage and for the repeal of DOMA.[^cite_note-NPR-2012a-25]

On May 9, 2012, [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) became the first sitting president to say he supports same-sex marriage.[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-The_Huffington_Post-268] Previously, he had opposed restrictions on same-sex marriage such as the [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act), which he promised to repeal,[^cite_note-LGBT-269] California's [Prop 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prop_8),[^cite_note-270] and a [constitutional amendment](261586.md) to ban same-sex marriage (which he opposed, saying that "decisions about marriage should be left to the states as they always have been"),[^cite_note-271] but also stated that he personally believed marriage to be between a man and a woman and that he favored civil unions that would "give same-sex couples equal legal rights and privileges as married couples".[^cite_note-LGBT-269] Earlier, when running for the Illinois Senate in 1996 he said, "I favor legalizing same-sex marriages, and would fight efforts to prohibit such marriages".[^cite_note-272] Former presidents [Bill Clinton](3356.md)[^cite_note-273] and [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter)[^cite_note-274] along with former Democratic presidential nominees [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore)[^cite_note-275] and [Michael Dukakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Dukakis)[^cite_note-276] support same-sex marriage. President [Joe Biden](145422.md) has supported [same-sex marriage](92656.md) since 2012, when he became the highest-ranking government official to support it. In 2022, Biden signed the [Respect for Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respect_for_Marriage_Act); the law repealed the Defense of Marriage Act, which Biden had voted for during his Senate tenure.[^cite_note-277]

#### Status of Puerto Rico and D.C.

The 2016 Democratic Party platform promised [debt restructuring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debt_restructuring) for the Puerto Rican government, Puerto Rican participation in presidential elections, and a system of government self-determined by Puerto Ricans insofar as it complies with national laws. The platform also proposes [statehood for the District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_statehood_movement).[^cite_note-2016platform-278]

### Legal issues

#### Gun control



&#x20;U.S. opinion on gun control issues is deeply divided along political lines, as shown in this 2021 survey.

[^cite_note-Pew_20210420-279]

With a stated goal of reducing crime and homicide, the Democratic Party has introduced various [gun control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_politics_in_the_United_States) measures, most notably the [Gun Control Act of 1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_Control_Act_of_1968), the [Brady Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brady_Handgun_Violence_Prevention_Act) of 1993 and the [Violent Crime Control and Law Enforcement Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violent_Crime_Control_and_Law_Enforcement_Act) (1994). In its national platform for [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Democratic_National_Convention), the only statement explicitly favoring gun control was a plan calling for renewal of the 1994 [Assault Weapons Ban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Assault_Weapons_Ban).[^cite_note-280] In 2022, Democratic president [Joe Biden](145422.md) signed the [Bipartisan Safer Communities Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bipartisan_Safer_Communities_Act), which, among other things, expanded background checks and provided incentives for states to pass [red flag laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_flag_laws).[^cite_note-281]

The Democratic Party does not oppose gun ownership.[^cite_note-282] According to a 2023 Pew Research Center poll, 20% of Democrats owned firearms, compared to 32% of the general public and 45% of Republicans.[^cite_note-283]

#### Death penalty

See also: [Capital punishment in the United States](412425.md)

The Democratic position on capital punishment has shifted multiple times over the decades. In 1968, Attorney General [Ramsey Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramsey_Clark), representing the [Johnson Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Lyndon_B._Johnson), asked Congress to abolish the [federal death penalty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_by_the_United_States_federal_government).[^cite_note-284] In [1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_Democratic_National_Convention), the Democratic Party platform called for the abolition of capital punishment.[^cite_note-1972-Platform-285] In 1988, Democratic presidential nominee [Michael Dukakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Dukakis)'s statement in the [1988 United States presidential debates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_United_States_presidential_debates) that he would oppose the death penalty even if his wife were raped and murdered was seen by many viewers as callous and emotionless, and was widely viewed as having contributed to his loss to [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) in the general election.[^cite_note-286]

During his [presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Clinton_1992_presidential_campaign), [Bill Clinton](3356.md) sought to distance himself from his party's left flank through his strong support for the death penalty, including by personally supervising the execution of [Ricky Ray Rector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricky_Ray_Rector), a lobotomized African-American man convicted of killing a police officer.[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288] During [Clinton's presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Bill_Clinton), Democrats led the expansion of the federal death penalty. These efforts were manifested in the 1994 [Violent Crime Control and Law Enforcement Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violent_Crime_Control_and_Law_Enforcement_Act), which expanded the federal death penalty to around 60 offenses, and the [Antiterrorism and Effective Death Penalty Act of 1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiterrorism_and_Effective_Death_Penalty_Act_of_1996), which heavily limited appeals in death penalty cases.[^cite_note-289] The Democratic Party platforms of [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Democratic_National_Convention) and [2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Democratic_National_Convention) supported capital punishment outright, while the Democratic Party platforms of 2008 and [2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Democratic_National_Convention) warned against arbitrary application and the execution of innocents.[^cite_note-290]

In June [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Democratic_National_Convention), the Democratic Platform Drafting Committee unanimously adopted an amendment to abolish the death penalty.[^cite_note-291] The 2020 Democratic Party platform reiterated the Party's opposition to capital punishment.[^cite_note-292] The [2024 platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Democratic_National_Convention) is the first since the [2004 platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Democratic_National_Convention) that does not mention the death penalty, and the first since 2016 not to call for abolition.[^cite_note-293] However, on December 23, 2024, President Biden commuted the sentences of 37 out of 40 [federal death row inmates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_death_row_inmates_in_the_United_States#Federal) to life in prison without parole.[^cite_note-294]

#### Torture

Many Democrats are opposed to the [use of torture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torture_and_the_United_States) against individuals apprehended and held prisoner by the [United States military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_armed_forces), and hold that categorizing such prisoners as [unlawful combatants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unlawful_combatant) does not release the United States from its obligations under the [Geneva Conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Conventions). Democrats contend that torture is inhumane, damages the United States' moral standing in the world, and produces questionable results. Democrats are largely against [waterboarding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterboarding).[^cite_note-295]

Torture became a divisive issue in the party after Barack Obama was elected president.[^cite_note-296]

#### Privacy

The Democratic Party believes that individuals should have a [right to privacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privacy_law). For example, many Democrats have opposed the [NSA warrantless surveillance of American citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NSA_warrantless_surveillance_\(2001%E2%80%932007\)).[^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298]

Some Democratic officeholders have championed [consumer-protection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_protection) laws that limit the sharing of consumer data among corporations. Democrats have opposed [sodomy laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodomy_laws_in_the_United_States) since the 1972 platform, which stated that "Americans should be free to make their own choice of life-styles and private habits without being subject to discrimination or prosecution",[^cite_note-1972-Platform-285] and believe that government should not regulate [consensual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consensual) noncommercial sexual conduct among adults as a matter of personal privacy.[^cite_note-299]

### Foreign policy issues

In foreign policy, the party supports [liberal internationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_internationalism) as well as tough stances against [China](5405.md) and [Russia](25391.md).[^cite_note-Ikenberry_2020-300][^cite_note-Wong_2022-301][^cite_note-The_World_Trump_Wants-302]

The foreign policy of the voters of the two major parties has largely overlapped since the 1990s. A Gallup poll in early 2013 showed broad agreement on the top issues, albeit with some divergence regarding human rights and international cooperation through agencies such as the United Nations.[^cite_note-303]

In June 2014, the Quinnipiac Poll asked Americans which foreign policy they preferred:

> A) The United States is doing too much in other countries around the world, and it is time to do less around the world and focus more on our own problems here at home. B) The United States must continue to push forward to promote democracy and freedom in other countries worldwide because these efforts make our own country more secure.

Democrats chose A over B by 65% to 32%; Republicans chose A over B by 56% to 39%; and independents chose A over B by 67% to 29%.[^cite_note-304]

#### Iran sanctions

See also: [United States sanctions against Iran](9941859.md)

The Democratic Party has been critical of [Iran's nuclear program](721807.md) and supported economic sanctions against the Iranian government. In 2013, the Democratic-led administration worked to reach a diplomatic agreement with the government of Iran to halt the Iranian nuclear program in exchange for [international economic sanction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions_against_Iran) relief.[^cite_note-305] As of 2014, negotiations had been successful and the party called for more cooperation with Iran in the future.[^cite_note-306] In 2015, the Obama administration agreed to the [Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Comprehensive_Plan_of_Action), which provides sanction relief in exchange for international oversight of the [Iranian nuclear program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_nuclear_program). In February 2019, the Democratic National Committee passed a resolution calling on the United States to re-enter the JCPOA, from which President Trump withdrew the United States in 2018.[^cite_note-307]

#### Invasion of Afghanistan

See also: [Afghanistan–United States relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93United_States_relations) and [International public opinion on the war in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_public_opinion_on_the_war_in_Afghanistan)

Democrats in the House of Representatives and in the Senate near-unanimously voted for the [Authorization for Use of Military Force Against Terrorists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authorization_for_Use_of_Military_Force_Against_Terrorists) against "those responsible for the [recent attacks launched against the United States](5058690.md)" in [Afghanistan](737.md) in 2001, supporting the [NATO](21133.md) coalition [invasion of the nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Enduring_Freedom). Most elected Democrats continued to support the [Afghanistan conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_\(2001%E2%80%93present\)) during George W. Bush's presidency.[^cite_note-308][^cite_note-daily-309] During the [2008 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election), then-candidate [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) called for a "surge" of troops into Afghanistan.[^cite_note-daily-309] After winning the presidency, Obama followed through, sending additional troops to Afghanistan. Troop levels were 94,000 in December 2011 and kept falling, with a target of 68,000 by fall 2012.[^cite_note-310]

Support for the war among the American people diminished over time. Many Democrats changed their opinion over the course of the war, coming to oppose the continuation of the conflict.[^cite_note-holland-311][^cite_note-edge-312] In July 2008, [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_poll) found that 41% of Democrats called the invasion a "mistake" while a 55% majority disagreed.[^cite_note-edge-312] A [CNN](62028.md) survey in August 2009 stated that a majority of Democrats opposed the war. CNN polling director Keating Holland said: "Nearly two thirds of Republicans support the war in Afghanistan. Three quarters of Democrats oppose the war".[^cite_note-holland-311]

During the [2020 presidential election](48410011.md), then-candidate [Joe Biden](145422.md) promised to "end the forever wars in Afghanistan and the Middle East."[^cite_note-313] Biden went on to win the election, and, in April 2021, he announced that he would withdraw all U.S. troops from Afghanistan by September 11 of that year.[^cite_note-314] The last troops left in August, bringing America's 20-year-long military campaign in the country to a close.[^cite_note-315] According to a 2023 AP-NORC poll, a majority of Democrats believed that the War in Afghanistan was not worth it.[^cite_note-316]

#### Israel

See also: [Israel–United States relations](3449549.md)



Israeli prime minister [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md) meeting with President Obama in 2013.

Democrats have historically been stronger supporters of Israel than Republicans.[^cite_note-Cavari-2020-317] During the 1940s, the party advocated for the cause of an independent Jewish state over the objections of many [conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States) in the [Old Right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Right_\(United_States\)), who strongly opposed it.[^cite_note-Cavari-2020-317] In 1948, Democratic President [Harry Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_S._Truman) became the first world leader to recognize an independent state of Israel.[^cite_note-318]

The 2020 Democratic Party platform acknowledges a "commitment to Israel's security, its qualitative military edge, its right to defend itself, and the 2016 Memorandum of Understanding is ironclad" and that "we oppose any effort to unfairly single out and delegitimize Israel, including at the United Nations or through the [Boycott, Divestment, and Sanctions Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boycott,_Divestment_and_Sanctions)".[^cite_note-319] During the [Gaza war](74998519.md), the party requested a large-scale military aid package to Israel.[^cite_note-320] Biden also announced [military support for Israel](65284610.md), condemned the actions of [Hamas](13913.md) and other Palestinian militants as terrorism,[^cite_note-321] and ordered the U.S. military to build a port to facilitate the arrival of [humanitarian aid to Palestinian civilians in Gaza](76066918.md).[^cite_note-322]

However, parts of the Democratic base also became more skeptical of the Israeli government.[^cite_note-323] The number of Democrats (and Americans in general) who oppose sending arms to Israel has grown as Israel's war in Gaza has continued.[^cite_note-324] Experts said support for Israel could have hurt Democrats in several key states, including Michigan and Pennsylvania, in the 2024 presidential election.[^cite_note-325]

Late in 2024, twenty Democratic lawmakers requested support for U.S. legislation that would ban arms trade with countries that hinder humanitarian aid.[^cite_note-326] According to a [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) poll conducted in March 2025, 69% of Democrats have an unfavorable view of Israel, compared to 53% in 2022, before the Gaza war.[^cite_note-327] By July 2025, about half of the Democratic Senate delegation was opposed to sending arms to Israel.[^cite_note-328] Within the context of sympathies in the Middle East situation, a [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup,_Inc.) poll conducted in February 2026 showed that 65% of Democrats expressed sympathy for Palestinians, compared to 17% for Israelis.[^cite_note-329][^cite_note-330]

#### Europe, Russia, and Ukraine

The 2022 [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) was politically and economically opposed by the Biden Administration, who promptly began an increased arming of Ukraine.[^cite_note-against_2022_05_19_thehill-331][^cite_note-loud_2023_05_19_nytimes-332] In October 2023, the Biden administration requested an additional \$61.4 billion in aid for Ukraine for the year ahead,[^cite_note-333] but delays in the passage of further aid by the Republican-controlled [House of Representatives](19468510.md) inhibited progress, with the additional \$61 billion in aid to Ukraine added in April 2024.[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336]

## Factions

Main article: [Factions in the Democratic Party (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Factions_in_the_Democratic_Party_\(United_States\))



Bar plot of the percentage of the population with a BA or higher in the electoral jurisdictions won by Kamala Harris in the 2024 United States presidential election.

[^cite_note-CensusData-337]

Upon foundation, the Democratic Party supported [agrarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarianism) and the [Jacksonian democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacksonian_democracy) movement of President [Andrew Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Jackson), representing farmers and rural interests and traditional [Jeffersonian democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffersonian_democracy).[^cite_note-338] Since the 1890s, especially in Northern states, the party began to favor more liberal positions (the term "liberal" in this sense describes [modern liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_liberalism_in_the_United_States), rather than [classical liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_liberalism) or [economic liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalism)). Historically, the party has represented farmers, laborers, and religious and ethnic minorities as it has opposed unregulated business and finance and favored progressive income taxes.

In the 1930s, the party began advocating [social programs](21109533.md) targeted at the poor. Before the [New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal), the party had a [fiscally conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_conservatism), [pro-business](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalism) wing, typified by [Grover Cleveland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grover_Cleveland) and [Al Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Smith).[^cite_note-339] The party was [dominant in the Southern United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solid_South) until President [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) signed the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964). In foreign policy, [internationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internationalism_\(politics\)) (including [interventionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interventionism_\(politics\))) was a dominant theme from 1913 to the mid-1960s. The major influences for liberalism were labor unions (which peaked in the 1936–1952 era) and African Americans. Environmentalism has been a major component since the 1970s.

Even after the New Deal, until the 2010s, the party still had [a fiscally conservative faction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_coalition),[^cite_note-blue-dog-regroup-340] such as [John Nance Garner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Nance_Garner) and [Howard W. Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_W._Smith).[^cite_note-341] The party's [Southern conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Democrats) wing began shrinking after President [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) supported the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964), and largely died out in the 2010s, as the Republican Party built up its Southern base.[^cite_note-The_long_goodbye-87][^cite_note-Cohn-2014-157] The party still receives support from African Americans and urban areas in the Southern United States.[^cite_note-342][^cite_note-343]

The 21st-century Democratic Party is predominantly a coalition of centrists, liberals, and progressives, with significant overlap between the three groups. In 2019, the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) found that among Democratic and Democratic-leaning registered voters, 47% identify as liberal or very liberal, 38% identify as moderate, and 14% identify as conservative or very conservative.[^cite_note-344][^cite_note-345] Political scientists characterize the Democratic Party as less ideologically cohesive than the Republican Party due to the broader diversity of coalitions that compose the Democratic Party.[^cite_note-Gidron-2019-346][^cite_note-Grossman-2016-347][^cite_note-Lelkes-2016-348]

The party has lost significant ground with voters without college degrees in the 21st century, in line with trends across the developed world. The realignment unfolded gradually, first with white voters in the [South](179553.md)[^cite_note-Cohn-2014-157][^cite_note-Dogs-158] and [Midwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern_United_States),[^cite_note-349] and later with voters as a whole without college degrees, except for [African Americans](2154.md).[^cite_note-Where_Have_All_The_Democrats_Gone?-350][^cite_note-351]

Democrats have consistently won voters with [graduate degrees](153981.md) since the 1990s, including a majority of white voters with graduate degrees.[^cite_note-Polarization_by_education-352] Since the 2010s, the party's main demographic gains have been among white voters with college degrees, which until 2016 had been a Republican-leaning group.[^cite_note-Harry_Enten-149] The party still receives extremely strong support from [African Americans](2154.md), but has lost ground among other racial minorities, including [Hispanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans), [Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States), and [Asian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans).[^cite_note-Lost_Their-353]

### Liberals

See also: [Modern liberalism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_liberalism_in_the_United_States)

Percent of self-identified liberals by state in 2018

Correlation between Educational Attainment and Liberalism by State

**Top:** Percent of self-identified liberals by state in 2018, according to a [Gallup poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_poll).

[^cite_note-Liberals-354]

&#x20;**Bottom:** Linear regression of educational attainment vs. liberalism by state, based on the same Gallup data.

[^cite_note-Liberals-354]

Modern liberals are a large portion of the Democratic base. According to 2018 exit polls, liberals constituted 27% of the electorate, and 91% of American liberals favored the candidate of the Democratic Party.[^cite_note-2018e-355] [White-collar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-collar_worker) college-educated professionals were mostly Republican until the 1950s, but they had become a vital component of the Democratic Party by the early 2000s.[^cite_note-Judis,_B._J._(July_11,_2003)._The_trouble_with_Howard_Dean._''Salon.com''.-356]

According to a 2026 [Gallup poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_poll), 35% of American voters identify as "conservative" or "very conservative", 33% as "moderate", and 28% as "liberal" or "very liberal". For Democrats, 8% identified as conservative, 32% as moderate, and 59% as liberal.[^cite_note-357]

A large majority of liberals favor moving toward [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care). A majority also favor diplomacy over [military action](33158.md); [stem cell research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stem_cell), [same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States), stricter [gun control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_politics_in_the_United_States), environmental protection laws, as well as the preservation of [abortion rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-choice). Immigration and [cultural diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_diversity) are deemed positive as liberals favor [cultural pluralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_pluralism), a system in which immigrants retain their native culture in addition to adopting their new culture. Most liberals oppose increased military spending and the mixing of church and state.[^cite_note-Pew_Research_Center.-358] As of 2020, the three most significant labor groupings in the Democratic coalition were the [AFL–CIO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AFL%E2%80%93CIO) and [Change to Win](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Change_to_Win_Federation) [labor federations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_trade_union_center) as well as the [National Education Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Education_Association), a large, unaffiliated teachers' union. Important issues for labor unions include supporting unionized manufacturing jobs, raising the [minimum wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage_in_the_United_States), and promoting broad social programs such as [Social Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Security_\(United_States\)) and [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(United_States\)).[^cite_note-359]

This ideological group is strongly correlated with high educational attainment. According to the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center), 49% were college graduates, the highest figure of any typographical group.[^cite_note-Polarized_by_Degrees-360] It was also the fastest-growing typological group since the late 1990s to the present.[^cite_note-Pew_Research_Center.-358] Liberals include most of the academia[^cite_note-Kurtz,_H._(March_29,_2005)._College_Faculties_A_Most_Liberal_Lot,_Study_Finds._''The_Washington_Post''.-361] and large portions of the professional class.[^cite_note-nymag.com-362]

### Moderates

See also: [New Democrats (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Democrats_\(United_States\)), [New Democrat Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Democrat_Coalition), and [Blue Dog Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Dog_Coalition)

Moderate Democrats, or [New Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Democrats_\(United_States\)), are an ideologically [centrist](39619035.md) faction within the Democratic Party that emerged after the victory of [Republican](32070.md) [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) in the [1988 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-363] Running as a New Democrat, Bill Clinton won the [1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_United_States_presidential_election) and [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_United_States_presidential_election) presidential elections.[^cite_note-364] They are an economically [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalism) and "[Third Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Way)" faction that dominated the party for around 20 years, until the beginning of [Obama's presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Barack_Obama).[^cite_note-blue-dog-regroup-340][^cite_note-vox.com-365] They are represented by organizations such as the [New Democrat Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Democrat_Network) and the [New Democrat Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Democrat_Coalition).

The [Blue Dog Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Dog_Coalition), the most [right-leaning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-right_politics) part of the party, was formed during the [104th Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/104th_United_States_Congress) to give members from the Democratic Party representing conservative-leaning districts a unified voice after the Democrats' loss of Congress in the [1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections) [Republican Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Revolution).[^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367][^cite_note-368] However, in the late 2010s and early 2020s, the coalition's focus shifted towards ideological [centrism](39619035.md). One of the most influential centrist groups was the [Democratic Leadership Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Leadership_Council) (DLC), a nonprofit organization that advocated centrist positions for the party. The DLC disbanded in 2011.[^cite_note-369]

Some Democratic elected officials have self-declared as being centrists, including former president Bill Clinton, former vice president [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore), Senator [Mark Warner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Warner), Kansas governor [Laura Kelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laura_Kelly), former senator [Jim Webb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Webb), and President [Joe Biden](145422.md).[^cite_note-Members_—_New_Democrat_Coalition-370][^cite_note-371] The New Democrat Network supports socially liberal and fiscally moderate Democratic politicians and is associated with the congressional [New Democrat Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Democrat_Coalition) in the House.[^cite_note-372] [Annie Kuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annie_Kuster) is the chair of the coalition,[^cite_note-Members_—_New_Democrat_Coalition-370] and former senator and president [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) was self-described as a New Democrat.[^cite_note-373] In the 21st century, some former Republican moderates have switched to the Democratic Party.[^cite_note-374][^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376] Several anti-Trump conservatives likewise ended up becoming Democrats.[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378]

### Progressives

See also: [Congressional Progressive Caucus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Progressive_Caucus), [Progressivism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism_in_the_United_States), [Social democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_democracy), and [Squad (U.S. Congress)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squad_\(U.S._Congress\))



[Bernie Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernie_Sanders) and [Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria_Ocasio-Cortez) during the [Fighting Oligarchy tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fighting_Oligarchy_tour).

Progressives are the most [left-leaning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-left_politics) faction in the party and support strong business regulations, [social programs](21109533.md), and [workers' rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers'_rights).[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380] In 2014, progressive Senator [Elizabeth Warren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Warren) set out "Eleven Commandments of Progressivism": tougher regulation on corporations; affordable education; scientific investment and environmentalism; [net neutrality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Net_neutrality); increased wages; equal pay for women; collective bargaining rights; defending social programs; [same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States); [immigration reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_reform_in_the_United_States); and unabridged access to reproductive healthcare.[^cite_note-381] The [Congressional Progressive Caucus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Progressive_Caucus) (CPC) is a caucus of progressive Democrats chaired by [Greg Casar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greg_Casar) of [Texas](29810.md).[^cite_note-382][^cite_note-Gerstle2022-383] Its members have included representatives [Dennis Kucinich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dennis_Kucinich) of [Ohio](22199.md), [John Conyers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Conyers) of [Michigan](18859.md), [Jim McDermott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_McDermott) of [Washington](13015878.md), [Barbara Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbara_Lee) of California, and Senator [Paul Wellstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Wellstone) of [Minnesota](19590.md). Senators [Tammy Baldwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tammy_Baldwin) of [Wisconsin](33127.md), [Mazie Hirono](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazie_Hirono) of Hawaii, and [Ed Markey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Markey) of [Massachusetts](1645518.md) were members of the caucus when in the House of Representatives. As of 2024, the CPC is the second-largest ideological caucus in the House Democratic Caucus by voting members, behind the [New Democrat Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Democrat_Coalition).[^cite_note-384][^cite_note-385] Senator [Bernie Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernie_Sanders) has often been viewed as a leader of the progressive movement;[^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388] he ran presidential campaigns in [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries) and [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries).[^cite_note-389] Other members of the progressive faction include the [Squad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squad_\(U.S._Congress\)), a [left-wing](18499.md) group.[^cite_note-Cornwell-390] A small number of progressives self-identify as [democratic socialists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_socialism),[^cite_note-BrowningNYT-391][^cite_note-392] although supporters and some academic experts describe their beliefs as being closer to European [social democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_democracy).[^cite_note-BrowningNYT-391]

[^cite_note-RegVoters-2]: &#x20;Only includes voters registered as party affiliates in the District of Columbia and the 32 states which allow [voter registration with a specific party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_registration_in_the_United_States#Party_affiliation). Voters can also register as "independent", which has risen over time. In the United States, voter registration determines eligibility to vote in internal party elections such as primaries, rather than dues-paying membership as in the rest of the world. Voter registration states include [Alaska](624.md), [Arizona](21883824.md), [Arkansas](1930.md), [California](5407.md), [Colorado](5399.md), [Connecticut](6466.md), [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware), [District of Columbia](108956.md), [Florida](18933066.md), [Idaho](14607.md), [Iowa](26810748.md), [Kansas](16716.md), [Kentucky](16846.md), [Louisiana](18130.md), [Maine](19977.md), [Maryland](18858.md), [Massachusetts](1645518.md), [Missouri](19571.md), [Nebraska](21647.md), [Nevada](21216.md), [New Hampshire](21134.md), [New Jersey](21648.md), [New Mexico](21649.md), [New York](8210131.md), [North Carolina](21650.md), [Oklahoma](22489.md), [Oregon](26811621.md), [Pennsylvania](23332.md), [South Dakota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Dakota), [Utah](31716.md), [West Virginia](32905.md) and [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming). The remaining states lean heavily Republican, so the total percentage of the population that's Democrat or Republican is much closer than these values depict.<sup>[\[398\]](#cite_note-414)</sup>

[^cite_note-SenateCaucus-7]: &#x20; 45 senators are members of the party; however, two [independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Democrat) senators, [Angus King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angus_King) and [Bernie Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernie_Sanders), caucus with the Democrats.

[^cite_note-8]: &#x20;[Grover Cleveland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grover_Cleveland) in [1884](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1884_United_States_presidential_election) and [1892](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1884_United_States_presidential_election)

[^cite_note-9]: &#x20;[Samuel Tilden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Tilden) in [1876](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1876_United_States_presidential_election) and Grover Cleveland in [1888](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1888_United_States_presidential_election)

[^cite_note-111]: &#x20;All three incumbents in the 20th century to withdraw or not seek reelection—Calvin Coolidge, Harry S. Truman, and Lyndon B. Johnson—had succeeded to the presidency when their predecessor died, then won a second term in their own right.<sup>[\[105\]](#cite_note-Klassen-2024-109)</sup> Three presidents in the 1800s made and kept pledges to serve only one term, most recently [Rutherford B. Hayes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rutherford_B._Hayes).<sup>[\[106\]](#cite_note-110)</sup>

[^cite_note-397]: &#x20;Elected as Vice President with the [National Union Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Union_Party_\(United_States\)) ticket in the 1864 presidential election. Ascended to the presidency after the assassination of President Abraham Lincoln in 1865. Rejoined the Democratic Party in 1868.

[^cite_note-died-398]: &#x20;Died in office

[^cite_note-405]: &#x20;Comparing seats held immediately preceding and following the general election

[^cite_note-406]: &#x20;Tyler was ejected from the Whig Party, in September 1841.

[^cite_note-tie1-407]: &#x20;Republican Vice President [Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cheney) provided a [tie-breaking vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tie-breaking_votes_cast_by_the_vice_president_of_the_United_States), giving Republicans a majority until June 6, 2001, when [Jim Jeffords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Jeffords) left Republicans to join the Democratic Caucus.

[^cite_note-408]: &#x20;While there was no official Democratic nominee, the majority of the Democratic electors still cast their electoral votes for incumbent Vice President [Richard Mentor Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Mentor_Johnson).

[^cite_note-409]: &#x20;Greeley and Brown were [cross-endorsed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_fusion_in_the_United_States) by the [Liberal Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Republican_Party_\(United_States\)).

[^cite_note-410]: &#x20;Although Tilden won a majority of the popular vote, Republican [Rutherford B. Hayes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rutherford_B._Hayes) won a majority of votes in the Electoral College.

[^cite_note-411]: &#x20;Although Cleveland won a [plurality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plurality_\(voting\)) of the popular vote, Republican [Benjamin Harrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Harrison) won a majority of votes in the Electoral College.

[^cite_note-412]: &#x20;Although Gore won a [plurality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plurality_\(voting\)) of the popular vote, Republican [George W. Bush](3414021.md) won a majority of votes in the Electoral College.

[^cite_note-413]: &#x20;Although Clinton won a [plurality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plurality_\(voting\)) of the popular vote, Republican [Donald Trump](4848272.md) won a majority of votes in the Electoral College.

By the 1840s, Whig and Democratic congressmen voted as rival blocs. Whigs supported and Democrats opposed a weak executive, a new Bank of the United States, a high tariff, distribution of land revenues to the states, relief legislation to mitigate the effects of the depression, and federal reapportionment of House seats. Whigs voted against and Democrats approved an independent treasury, an aggressive foreign policy, and expansionism. These were important issues, capable of dividing the electorate just as they divided the major parties in Congress.

[S2CID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S2CID_\(identifier\)) [154860857](https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID\:154860857). Events surrounding the presidential election of 1964 marked a watershed in terms of the parties and the South (Pomper, 1972). The Solid South was built around the identification of the Democratic party with the cause of white supremacy. Events before 1964 gave white southerners pause about the linkage between the Democratic Party and white supremacy, but the 1964 election, passage of the Civil Rights Act of 1964, and the Voting Rights Act of 1965 altered in the minds of most the positions of the national parties on racial issues.

[^cite_note-Black-2003-70]: &#x20;Black, Earl; Black, Merle (September 30, 2003). [*The Rise of Southern Republicans*](http://www.hup.harvard.edu/catalog.php?isbn=9780674012486). Harvard University Press. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [9780674012486](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/9780674012486). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20180612135934/http://www.hup.harvard.edu/catalog.php?isbn=9780674012486) from the original on June 12, 2018. Retrieved June 9, 2018. When the Republican party nominated Arizona Senator Barry Goldwater—one of the few senators who had opposed the Civil Rights Act—as their presidential candidate in 1964, the party attracted many southern whites but permanently alienated African-American voters. Beginning with the Goldwater-versus-Johnson campaign more southern whites voted Republican than Democratic, a pattern that has recurred in every subsequent presidential election. ... Before the 1964 presidential election the Republican party had not carried any Deep South state for eighty-eight years. Yet shortly after Congress passed the Civil Rights Act, hundreds of Deep South counties gave Barry Goldwater landslide majorities.

[S2CID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S2CID_\(identifier\)) [12885628](https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID\:12885628). By 2000, however, the New Deal party alignment no longer captured patterns of partisan voting. In the intervening 40 years, the Civil Rights and Voting Rights Acts had triggered an increasingly race-driven distinction between the parties. ... Goldwater won the electoral votes of five states of the Deep South in 1964, four of them states that had voted Democratic for 84 years (Califano 1991, 55). He forged a new identification of the Republican party with racial conservatism, reversing a century-long association of the GOP with racial liberalism. This in turn opened the door for Nixon's "Southern strategy" and the Reagan victories of the eighties.

The version of neoliberalism embedded in these policies understood a distinct role for government to stimulate market-oriented solutions to address social ills such as unemployment and poverty. It thereby aimed not to eradicate the welfare state but rather to reformulate it. It extended the importance of poverty alleviation, which had long served as a benchmark of liberal policy, and had many similarities with the basic ideas of the war on poverty.

The statistic that best defines our politics over the past 20 years is this: Nine of the past ten national elections have resulted in a change in power in at least one chamber of Congress or the White House. (The sole outlier is 2012.) Several of those elections were considered at the time to be realignments that would lead to a sustained majority for one of the major parties. ... After Republicans defeated John Kerry in 2004 and snatched five Senate seats across the South, commentators believed social issues like gay marriage would set an unwinnable trap for Democrats. Hugh Hewitt wrote a book called Painting the Map Red, imagining a permanent conservative majority. Democrats then took the House and Senate in the 2006 midterms. When Barack Obama crushed John McCain in post–financial crisis 2008, Democratic pundits decided they had an enduring majority. The Tea Party thrashed them in 2010. The conventional wisdom was that Obama was toast; he won in 2012. Donald Trump's 2016 victory signaled a changed electorate, until Democrats won the House in 2018 and the presidency in 2020, only to lose both in 2022 and 2024.

Retrieved August 10, 2026. The Democratic Party styles itself a fighter for the working class. But a substantial part of that class-the white part-wants nothing to do with it. Still, as Democrats debate whether to follow Bernie Sanders's advice and move to the left on economics with the goal of winning back working-class white voters, it is surely at least worth noting that the two Senate candidates most identified with that strategy who ran this year did worse than Hillary Clinton despite her much-discussed weaknesses, and the two Senate candidates who tried hardest to frame themselves as moderates did better than her in their respective states.

[^cite_note-Abramowitz_2021-156]: &#x20;Abramowitz, Alan I. (September 23, 2021). Retrieved February 7, 2022. The data in Table 3 show that ideology had a powerful influence on vote choice in the 2020 presidential election. Almost all white voters located to the left of center on the ideology scale, regardless of education, voted for Biden, while almost all white voters located to the right of center, regardless of education, voted for Trump. Those in the center, just over one-fifth of white voters, favored Biden overall by a margin of 57% to 43%. However, there is little evidence that economic insecurity had any impact on the candidate preferences of even this group. Finally, it is worth noting that after controlling for ideology, there is almost no remaining difference between the candidate preferences of college and non-college whites. The class divide in candidate preference among white voters in 2020 is almost entirely explained by the fact that non-college white voters are now far more conservative across the board than are white college graduates.

Retrieved June 24, 2024. President Obama's landslide victory in 2008 was supposed to herald the beginning of a new Democratic era. And yet, six years later, there is not even a clear Democratic majority in the country, let alone one poised for 30 years of dominance. It's not because Mr. Obama's so-called new coalition of young and nonwhite voters failed to live up to its potential. They again turned out in record numbers in 2012. The Democratic majority has failed to materialize because the Republicans made large, countervailing and unappreciated gains of their own among white Southerners.

[^cite_note-Dogs-158]: &#x20;Kilgore, Ed (November 10, 2014). ["From Yellow Dogs To Blue Dogs To New Dogs"](https://washingtonmonthly.com/2014/11/10/from-yellow-dogs-to-blue-dogs-to-new-dogs/). *[Washington Monthly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Monthly)*. Retrieved December 24, 2016. Even more to the point, once the ancient white Democratic voting habits were broken, there was really no going back. Blue Dogs were a fading echo of the Yellow Dog tradition in the South, in which the Democratic Party was the default vehicle for day-to-day political life, and the dominant presence, regardless of ideology, for state and local politics. ... So Martin's right: the Blue Dog model is gone for good. But I would warn against the very popular assumption that Democrats can simply intone "economic populism" and regain traction among "the economically pressed white voter" of the Deep South. All the reasons Democrats are struggling with non-college-educated white voters nationally are especially strong in the South: racial and religious fears, anti-urbanism, militarism, and mistrust of unions as well as Wall Street. And for a whole host of reasons, including exceptionally weak union affiliation levels and a neo-colonial heritage as a region starved for capital, the Deep South is going to be more "pro-business" than most of the country.

Retrieved February 13, 2026. Democrats (87%) and Independents (79%) remain more likely to view international trade as beneficial to the United States than are Republicans (68%). Notably, views of trade as a benefit for the country are up among all partisan groups compared to eight years ago (by 16 points for Democrats, 27 points for Independents, and 22 points for Republicans). In relative terms, trade skepticism is far more prevalent among Republicans than Democrats, though it is low overall. Whereas 16 percent of Americans overall think the benefits of trade accrue mostly to other countries, close to three in 10 Republicans (28%) hold this view, echoing Trump's oft-repeated gripe that the United States is being "ripped off" by its trading partners worldwide, regardless of whether they are US allies. By contrast, only 8 percent of Democrats feel the same way.

Retrieved February 13, 2026. Views of U.S. free trade agreements are more positive among Democratic and Democratic-leaning respondents (60% good thing vs. 30% bad thing) than Republican and Republican-leaners (40% good thing vs. 52% bad thing). By a 58% to 31% margin, more Clinton supporters among registered Democrats say free trade agreements have been a good thing than a bad thing for the U.S. Views among Democratic backers of Sanders are similar (55% good thing vs. 38% bad thing).

Retrieved February 2, 2023. This was not merely a geographic shift, trading one region for another, but a more fundamental transformation of the anti-abortion movement's political ideology. In 1973 many of the most vocal opponents of abortion were northern Democrats who believed in an expanded social-welfare state and who wanted to reduce abortion rates through prenatal insurance and federally funded day care. In 2022, most anti-abortion politicians are conservative Republicans who are skeptical of such measures. What happened was a seismic religious and political shift in opposition to abortion that has not occurred in any other Western country.

[^cite_note-The_World_Trump_Wants-302]: &#x20;Kimmage, Michael (February 25, 2025). ["The World Trump Wants: American Power in the New Age of Nationalism"](https://www.foreignaffairs.com/united-states/world-trump-wants-michael-kimmage). *Foreign Affairs*. Vol. 104, no. 2. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20250317132352/https://www.foreignaffairs.com/united-states/world-trump-wants-michael-kimmage) from the original on March 17, 2025. Retrieved March 18, 2025. In the two decades that followed the Cold War's end, globalism gained ground over nationalism. Simultaneously, the rise of increasingly complex systems and networks—institutional, financial, and technological—overshadowed the role of the individual in politics. But in the early 2010s, a profound shift began. By learning to harness the tools of this century, a cadre of charismatic figures revived the archetypes of the previous one: the strong leader, the great nation, the proud civilization. ... They are self-styled strongmen who place little stock in rules-based systems, alliances, or multinational forums. They embrace the once and future glory of the countries they govern, asserting an almost mystical mandate for their rule. **Although their programs can involve radical change, their political strategies rely on strains of conservatism, appealing over the heads of liberal, urban, cosmopolitan elites to constituencies animated by a hunger for tradition and a desire for belonging.**

[^cite_note-349]: &#x20;Cuenco, Michael (August 21, 2024). ["Is Obama the reason Democrats are now 'underdogs'?"](https://unherd.com/newsroom/is-obama-the-reason-democrats-are-now-underdogs/). *[UnHerd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UnHerd)*. Retrieved December 10, 2024. Consider that when Obama last ran, the Midwest was still known as an impenetrable Blue Wall, while Florida and Ohio were still purple states. When Bill Clinton gave his acceptance speech in 1996, the Democrats were competitive throughout large swathes of the South. During that period, they had gone on to win not just Clinton's Arkansas and Al Gore's Tennessee, but states such as Kentucky and Louisiana too. The story of the last three decades has been one of political success for Democrats, who have won the popular vote in seven out of the last nine elections. Yet it is also one of narrowing political constituencies and pyrrhic victories, as the party attracted college-educated professionals at the expense of the non-college-educated majority. In particular, non-college-educated whites were lost, but in recent years they have increasingly been joined by significant numbers of non-college-educated minorities.

[^cite_note-Where_Have_All_The_Democrats_Gone?-350]: &#x20;Morris, G. Elliott; Brown, Amina; Marriner, Katie. ["Where Have All The Democrats Gone?"](https://web.archive.org/web/20250204231156/https://projects.fivethirtyeight.com/2024-election-swings/). *[FiveThirtyEight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FiveThirtyEight)*. Archived from [the original](https://projects.fivethirtyeight.com/2024-election-swings/) on February 4, 2025. This all raises questions about Democrats' messaging about the economy, or maybe suggests that the type of people they hypothesize would be helped most by their policies — such as wealth redistribution from progressive and corporate taxation and federal subsidies for companies that invest in underdeveloped areas, especially when it comes to manufacturing — are not as responsive as the party hoped to the type of so-called policy "deliverism" that the Obama and Biden administrations pursued. In a more dire framing for Democrats: If a party that tells itself it stands for working-class voters is systematically losing support with those people, something has gone terribly wrong for them.

[^cite_note-351]: &#x20;Kondik, Kyle (December 5, 2024). ["The End of the Line for Red State Senate Democrats"](https://centerforpolitics.org/crystalball/the-end-of-the-line-for-red-state-senate-democrats/). *[Sabato's Crystal Ball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabato's_Crystal_Ball)*. Retrieved January 17, 2025. As part of capturing the Senate this year, Republicans knocked out the final remaining Democratic senators from a group of 20 states that have consistently voted Republican for president since at least the 2000 election. A quarter-century ago, Democrats held nearly a third of the Senate seats from these 20 states. But that tally was down to just 2 leading into this election, and Republican victories in Montana and West Virginia reduced it to 0.

[^cite_note-Polarization_by_education-352]: &#x20;["Polarisation by education is remaking American politics"](https://www.economist.com/united-states/2024/10/13/polarisation-by-education-is-remaking-american-politics). *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)*. October 13, 2024. From 1952 to 2000, a majority of white voters with college degrees self-identified as Republicans. Starting with the 2012 election, this affiliation began to weaken. It loosened even more once \[Donald] Trump became the Republican standard-bearer in 2016. By 2020, the college-educated called themselves Democrats by a 2\:1 margin. And there were many more of them; their share of the electorate rose from 8% in 1952 to 40% in 2020. Had the party held on to the rest of its support, this would have ensured an enduring majority. Yet at the same time, Democrats lost support among whites without college degrees. They now favour Republicans by their own margin of 2\:1.

[^cite_note-Lost_Their-353]: &#x20;Cohn, Nate (November 25, 2024). ["How Democrats Lost Their Base and their Message"](https://www.nytimes.com/2024/11/25/upshot/democrats-trump-working-class.html). *[The New York Times](30680.md)*. Retrieved November 25, 2024. Donald Trump's populist pitch bumped Democrats off their traditional place in American politics. ... It has long been clear that the rise of Donald J. Trump meant the end of the Republican Party as we once knew it. It has belatedly become clear that his rise may have meant the end of the Democratic Party as we knew it as well. After three Trump elections, almost every traditional Democratic constituency has swung to the right. In fact, Mr. Trump has made larger gains among Black, Hispanic, Asian American, and young voters in his three campaigns since 2016 than he has among white voters without a college degree, according to New York Times estimates. In each case, Mr. Trump fared better than any Republican in decades. ... The overarching pattern is clear. In election after election, Democrats underperformed among traditional Democratic constituencies during the Trump era. Sometimes, it was merely a failure to capitalize on his unpopularity. Other times, it was a staggering decline in support. Together, it has shattered Democratic dreams of building a new majority with the rise of a new generation of young and nonwhite voters. This overarching pattern requires an overarching explanation: Mr. Trump's populist conservatism corroded the foundations of the Democratic Party's appeal. It tapped into many of the issues and themes that once made these voters Democrats.

In the United States, the policies that self-described democratic socialists advocate for generally do not involve the complete abolition of capitalism, but rather working within the system to enact left-wing priorities, such as raising the minimum wage.
